**Sikhism**,[^cite_note-8] also known as **Sikhi**,[^cite_note-9] is an [Indian religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_religions) and [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_philosophy)[^cite_note-\:2a-10] that originated in the [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab) region of the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) around the end of the 15th century CE. It is one of the most recently founded [major religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_religious_groups) and is followed by 25–30 million adherents, known as [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs).

Sikhism developed from the spiritual teachings of [Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak) (1469–1539), the faith's first [guru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru), and the nine [Sikh gurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_gurus) who succeeded him. The tenth guru, [Guru Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh) (1666–1708), named the [Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib), which is the central religious scripture in Sikhism, as his successor. This brought the line of human gurus to a close. Sikhs regard the Guru Granth Sahib as the 11th and eternally living guru.

The core beliefs and practices of Sikhism, articulated in the Guru Granth Sahib and other Sikh scriptures, include faith and meditation in the name of the one creator (*[Ik Onkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ik_Onkar)*), the divine unity and equality of all humankind, engaging in selfless service to others (*[sevā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sev%C4%81)*), striving for justice for the benefit and prosperity of all (*[sarbat da bhala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbat_da_bhala)*), and honest conduct and livelihood. Following this standard, Sikhism rejects claims that any particular religious tradition has a monopoly on absolute truth. As a consequence, Sikhs do not actively [proselytise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proselytism), although voluntary converts are generally accepted. Sikhism emphasises meditation and remembrance as a means to feel God's presence (*[simran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simran)*), which can be expressed musically through *[kirtan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirtan)* or internally through [*naam japna*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naam_Japo) (lit. 'meditation on God's name'). Baptised Sikhs are obliged to wear the [five Ks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Ks), which are five articles of faith which physically distinguish Sikhs from non-Sikhs. Among these include the *[kesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kesh_\(Sikhism\))* (uncut hair). Most religious Sikh men thus do not cut their hair but rather [wear a turban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dastar).[^cite_note-11]

> The definition of a Sikh, according to the *[Rehat Maryada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehat_Maryada)*, the Sikh code of conduct, is any human being who faithfully believes in the following:[^cite_note-12]
>
> 1. One Immortal Being,
>
> 2. Ten Gurus, from Guru Nanak Sahib to Guru Gobind Singh Sahib,
>
> 3. The Guru Granth Sahib,
>
> 4. The utterances and teachings of the ten Gurus and,
>
> 5. The baptism bequeathed by the tenth Guru;
>
> &#x20;and who does not owe allegiance to any other religion.

[Tarn Taran Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarn_Taran_Sahib) – The World's Largest Sarovar (sacred pool)

The religion developed and evolved in times of [religious persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_persecution#Persecutions_of_Sikhs), gaining converts from both [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) and [Islam](6037917.md). The [Mughal emperors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_emperor) of India tortured and executed two of the Sikh gurus—[Guru Arjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan) (1563–1606) and [Guru Tegh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Tegh_Bahadur) (1621–1675)—after [they refused to convert to Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_conversion#Islam). The persecution of the Sikhs triggered the founding of the *[Khalsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa)* by Guru Gobind Singh in 1699 as an order to protect the [freedom of conscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_conscience) and [religion](46006.md), with members expressing the qualities of a *[sant-sipāhī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sant_Sipahi)* ("saint-soldier").

## Terminology

The majority of Sikh scriptures were originally written in the alphabet of [*Gurmukhī*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmukhi), a script standardised by [Guru Angad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Angad) out of [Laṇḍā scripts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La%E1%B9%87%E1%B8%8D%C4%81_scripts) historically used in present-day Pakistan and [North India](1552939.md).[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-ShackleMandair2013pxxi-14] Adherents of Sikhism are known as *[Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs)*, meaning "students" or "disciples" of the guru. The [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) word *Sikhism* derives from the Punjabi word for the religion *Sikhi* ([Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language): ਸਿੱਖੀ *_Sikkhī_*, [\[ˈsɪk.kʰiː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Punjabi) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/84/Sikkhi.ogg/Sikkhi.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/8/84/Sikkhi.ogg/Sikkhi.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>, from [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language): ਸਿੱਖ, romanised: *[Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh)*, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'disciple'),[^cite_note-15] which connotes the "temporal path of learning" and is rooted in the verb *sikhana* (lit. 'to learn').[^cite_note-Mandair2013p12-16][^cite_note-17] The Global Institute of Sikh Studies translates the term *Sikh* as meaning "bearers of *sikhiā* \[wisdom]".[^cite_note-\:122-18]

Some Sikhs oppose the exonym term *Sikhism* as they claim the word was coined [by the British colonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) rather than by Sikhs themselves, and they instead prefer the endonym *Sikhi*. They argue that an "-ism" connotes a fixed and immutable worldview which is not congruent with the internally fluid nature of the Sikh philosophy.[^cite_note-Mandair2013p4-19] Other terms Sikhs use to name their faith are *gursikhi*, *[gurmat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmat)*, or *dharam*.[^cite_note-20]

## Philosophy and teachings

The basis of Sikhism lies in the teachings of [Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak) and his successors.[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22] [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh) emphasise the congruence between spiritual development and everyday moral conduct. Its founder, Guru Nanak, summarised this perspective by saying, "Truth is the highest virtue, but higher still is truthful living."[^cite_note-\:0-23]<sup>: 234 </sup> Sikhism emphasises *Ėk nūr te sab jag upjiā*, 'From the one light, the entire universe welled up.'[^cite_note-24] Guru Nanak also emphasised his teachings to his disciples by giving them real-life examples.

### God

See also: [Ik Onkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ik_Onkar) and [Waheguru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waheguru)

Sikhism is a [monotheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism) and [panentheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panentheism) religion. Sikhs believe that only one God exists and that God is simultaneously within everything and is all-encompassing. The oneness of God is reflected by the phrase *[Ik Onkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ik_Onkar)*.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26] In Sikhism, the word for God is *[Waheguru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waheguru)* (lit. 'wondrous teacher'). The *Waheguru* is considered to be *[Nirankar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirankar)* ("shapeless"), *Akal* ("timeless"), *Karta Purakh* ("[the creator being](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creator_deity)"), *Akaal Purkh* ("beyond time and death") and *Agam Agochar* ("[incomprehensible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_incomprehensibility) and invisible").[^cite_note-27]

In a literal sense, God has no [gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_of_God_in_Sikhism) in Sikhism, but metaphorically, God is presented as masculine and God's power as feminine. For example, Guru Gobind Singh refers to God as his father and God's creative power as his mother. Similarly, another example is that the *Guru Granth Sahib*, the primary Sikh scripture, says that all humans are soul-brides who long to unite with their husband Lord.[^cite_note-28] In addition, the gurus also wrote in the Guru Granth Sahib that there are many worlds on which the transcendental God has created life.[^cite_note-29]

The Sikh scripture begins with God as *Ik Onkar* (ੴ), the 'One Creator',[^cite_note-singhaikonkar-30][^cite_note-\:0-23]<sup>: 227 </sup> understood in the Sikh tradition as monotheistic unity of God.[^cite_note-31] *Ik onkar* (sometimes capitalised) is more loosely rendered 'the one supreme reality', 'the one creator', 'the all-pervading spirit', and other ways of expressing a diffused but unified and singular sense of God and creation.[^cite_note-32]

The traditional *[Mul Mantar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mul_Mantar)* goes from *ik onkar* until *Nanak hosee bhee sach.* The opening line of the *Guru Granth Sahib* and each subsequent *[raga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raga)* mentions *ik onkar*:[^cite_note-pashauramulmantar2-33]

> ੴ
>

> "There is one supreme being, the eternal reality, the creator, without fear and devoid of enmity, immortal, never incarnated, self-existent, known by grace through the true guru."

— *[Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib)* (17th c.), p. 1

### Worldly illusion



A sketch made in 1844 by [Emily Eden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emily_Eden) of the "Akalees or Immortals". Digitised by the [Panjab Digital Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panjab_Digital_Library).

*[Māyā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_\(illusion\))*, defined as a temporary illusion or "[unreality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derealization)", is one of the core deviations from the pursuit of God and salvation: where worldly attractions give only illusory, temporary satisfaction and pain that distracts from the process of the devotion of God. However, [Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak) emphasised māyā as not a reference to the world's unreality but its values. In Sikhism, the influences of [ego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahankar), [anger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krodh), [greed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobh), [attachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moh), and [lust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kam), known as the *pānj chor* ('[Five Thieves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Thieves)'), are believed to be particularly distracting and hurtful. Sikhs believe the world is currently in a state of *[Kali Yuga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kali_Yuga)* ('age of darkness') because the world is led astray by the love of and attachment to *māyā*.[^cite_note-34] The fate of people vulnerable to the five thieves is separation from God, and the situation may be remedied only after intensive and relentless devotion.[^cite_note-35]

### Timeless truth



A [Nihang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihang) Sikh at the [Harmandir Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmandir_Sahib), also called the [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple)

According to Guru Nanak, the supreme purpose of human life is to reconnect with *Akal* ('The Timeless One'). However, [egotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egotism) is the most significant barrier to making this connection. Using the Guru's teaching remembrance of *[nām](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81m_Jap%C5%8D)* (the divine Name of the Lord)[^cite_note-Pruthi_2004_204-36][^cite_note-NaamSimran-37] leads to the end of egotism. Guru Nanak designated the word *Guru* ('teacher')[^cite_note-38] to mean the voice of "the spirit": the source of knowledge and the guide to salvation.[^cite_note-p254-39] As *ik onkar* is [universally immanent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panentheism), *Guru* is indistinguishable from *Akal* and are one and the same.[^cite_note-singh2013-40]

### Liberation

Guru Nanak's teachings are founded not on a final destination of heaven or hell but on a spiritual union with the *Akal*, which results in [salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvation) or *[jivanmukti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jivanmukta)* ('enlightenment/liberation within one's lifetime'),[^cite_note-41] a concept also found in [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism).[^cite_note-Grewal-42] [Guru Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh) makes it clear that human birth is obtained with great fortune, and therefore one needs to be able to make the most of this life.[^cite_note-chahal2011-43]

Sikhs accept [reincarnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reincarnation) and [karma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma) concepts found in [Buddhism](3267529.md), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), and [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism),[^cite_note-Reichberg2014p672-44][^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46] but do not necessarily infer a [metaphysical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics) [soteriology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soteriology) such as a state of "heaven" or "nirvana."[^cite_note-chahal2011-43][^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] Nevertheless, in Sikhism, both karma and liberation are "modified by the concept of God's grace" (*nadar, mehar, kirpa, karam*, etc.).[^cite_note-Grewal-42] Guru Nanak states that "the body takes birth because of karma, but salvation is attained through grace."[^cite_note-49] To get closer to God, Sikhs: avoid the evils of *maya*; keep the everlasting truth in mind; practice *[shabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabad_\(hymn\)) [kirtan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirtan)* (musical recitation of hymns); [meditate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meditate) on *[naam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81m_Jap%C5%8D)*; and serve humanity. Sikhs believe that being in the company of the *[satsang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satsang)* (association with *sat*, 'true', people) or *sadh sangat* is one of the key ways to achieve liberation from the cycles of reincarnation.[^cite_note-50] The [Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs) community may be seen to correspond to [A.D. Smith's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_D._Smith) definition of a politicised community, sharing common ancestry myths and historical memories of [martyrdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyr) and persecution under successive rulers.[^cite_note-Shani_1–17-51]

### Power and devotion (Miri and Piri)

Main article: [Miri Piri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miri_Piri)

**Miri Piri** is a doctrine practiced in the Sikh religion since the seventeenth century. The doctrine of the "Mir" (social and political aspects of life) and the "Pir" (guides to the spiritual aspect of life) was revealed by the first guru of Sikhism, [Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak), but propounded by the sixth guru of Sikhism, [Guru Hargobind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Hargobind), on 12 June 1606.[^cite_note-Marty-52][^cite_note-jas-53][^cite_note-grove-54] After the martyrdom of [his father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan), Guru Hargobind was elevated to the Guruship and fulfilled the prophecy that was given by the primal figure of Sikh, [Baba Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_Buddha), that the guru will possess spiritual and temporal power. [Guru Hargobind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Hargobind) introduced the two swords of Miri and Piri, symbolising both worldly (social and political) and spiritual authority.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-jas-53] The two [kirpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirpan) of Miri and Piri are tied together with a [khanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanda_\(sword\)) in the centre, so the combination of both is considered supreme. This means that all action informed or arising out of the spiritual heart completes one's purpose and meaning in the world of action: [spirituality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality).[^cite_note-jas-53][^cite_note-56]

[Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak), the first Sikh guru and the founder of Sikhism, was a [Bhakti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti) saint.[^cite_note-richard-57] He taught that the most important form of worship is *Bhakti* (devotion to [Waheguru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waheguru)).[^cite_note-Mayled2002-58] [Guru Arjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan), in the *[Sukhmani Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhmani_Sahib)*, recommended the true religion as one of loving devotion to God.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60] The *[Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib)* includes suggestions on how a Sikh should perform constant Bhakti.[^cite_note-Mayled2002-58][^cite_note-sggs305306-61][^cite_note-Jhutti-Johal2011-62] Some scholars call Sikhism a Bhakti sect of Indian traditions,[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-davidlorenzen-64] adding that it emphasises "*nirguni Bhakti*", i.e. loving devotion to a divine without qualities or physical form.[^cite_note-davidlorenzen-64]<sup>: 1–3 </sup>[^cite_note-hardip-65][^cite_note-66] However, Sikhism also accepts the concept of *saguni*, i.e. a divine with qualities and form.[^cite_note-67] While Western scholarship generally places Sikhism as arising primarily within a Hindu Bhakti movement milieu while recognising some [Sufi Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism) influences,[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-\:1-69]<sup>: 3, 42–3 </sup> some Indian Sikh scholars disagree and state that Sikhism transcended the environment it emerged from. The basis of the latter analysis is that Bhakti traditions did not clearly disassociate from Vedic texts and their cosmologies and metaphysical worldview, while the Sikh tradition clearly did disassociate from the Vedic tradition.[^cite_note-Singha-70]

Several Sikh sects outside the [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_India) region of India, such as those found in [Maharashtra](20629.md) and [Bihar](442482.md), practice *[aarti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aarti)* (the ceremonial use of lamps) during Bhakti observances in a Sikh [gurdwara](482849.md).[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] However, most Sikh gurdwaras forbid *aarti* during their Bhakti practices.[^cite_note-\:1-69]<sup>: 201 </sup> While emphasising Bhakti, the Sikh gurus also taught that the spiritual life and secular householder life are intertwined, and not separate. This logically follows from the panentheistic nature of Sikh philosophy.[^cite_note-Kamala1-73] In Sikh worldview, the everyday world is part of the Infinite Reality, increased spiritual awareness leads to increased and vibrant participation in the everyday world.[^cite_note-Nikky1-74] Guru Nanak described living an "active, creative, and practical life" of "truthfulness, fidelity, self-control and purity" as being higher than the [metaphysical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics) truth.[^cite_note-Marwha1-75]

The sixth guru, Guru Hargobind, after Guru Arjan's martyrdom, faced with oppression by the Islamic [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), affirmed the philosophy that the political/temporal (*Miri*) and spiritual (*Piri*) realms are mutually coexistent.[^cite_note-Marty1-76][^cite_note-Gandhi1-77] According to the ninth Sikh guru, [Tegh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tegh_Bahadur), the ideal Sikh should have both *[Shakti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shakti)* (power that resides in the temporal), and *Bhakti* (spiritual meditative qualities). This was developed into the concept of the "saint soldier" by the tenth Sikh guru, [Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gobind_Singh).[^cite_note-Gandhi1-77]

The concept of humanity, as elaborated by Guru Nanak, refines and negates the "monotheistic concept of self/God", declaring that "monotheism becomes almost redundant in the movement and crossings of love".[^cite_note-Mandair1-78] Sikh gurus have taught that the human's goal is to end all dualities of "self and other, I and not-I", attain the "attendant balance of separation-fusion, self-other, action-inaction, attachment-detachment, in the course of daily life".[^cite_note-Mandair1-78]

### Singing and music

Sikhs refer to the hymns of the gurus as *[Gurbani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurbani)* (lit. 'Guru's word'). [Shabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabad_\(hymn\)) [Kirtan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirtan) is the singing of Gurbani. The entire [Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib) is written in the form of poetry and rhyme to be recited in thirty-one ragas of classical Indian music as specified. However, the exponents of these are rarely to be found amongst the Sikhs who are conversant with all the Ragas in the Guru Granth Sahib. [Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak) started the Shabad Kirtan tradition and taught that listening to kirtan is a powerful way to achieve tranquility while meditating, and singing of the glories of the "Supreme Timeless One" (God) with devotion is the most effective way to come in communion with the "Supreme Timeless One".[^cite_note-79] The three morning prayers for Sikhs consist of [Japji Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japji_Sahib), [Jaap Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaap_Sahib), and [Tav-Prasad Savaiye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tav-Prasad_Savaiye).[^cite_note-diaspora-80] Baptised Sikhs ([Amritdharis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amritdharis)) rise early and meditate, then recite all the [Five Banis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Banis) of Nitnem, before breakfast. The Five Banis consist of Japji Sahib, Jaap Sahib, Tav-Prasad Savaiye, [Chaupai Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaupai_Sahib), [Anand Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anand_Sahib); recitation of the banis *[paath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paath)* is followed by [Ardās](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ard%C4%81s), in which the [Sarbat da Bhala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbat_da_Bhala) principle is taught by gurus.[^cite_note-Kalsi_Chelsea-81][^cite_note-Cole_Sambhi-82][^cite_note-Teece_2004_4-83]

### Remembrance of the Divine Name

A key practice by Sikhs is remembrance[^cite_note-NaamSimran-37] of the *Naam* (divine name), Waheguru.[^cite_note-Pruthi_2004_204-36] This contemplation is done through *[Nām Japō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81m_Jap%C5%8D)* (repetition of the Divine Name) or *[Simran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simran)* (remembrance of the Divine Name through recitation).[^cite_note-NaamSimran-37][^cite_note-84] The verbal repetition of the name of God—or a sacred syllable—is an established practice in religious traditions in India; however, Sikhism developed *Naam-simran* as an important Bhakti practice.[^cite_note-Dhillon1988-85][^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87] Guru Nanak's ideal is the total exposure of one's being to the Divine Name and a conformation to [Dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma), the "Divine Order". Nanak described the result of the disciplined application of *nām simraṇ* as a "growing towards and into God" through a gradual process of five stages. The last of these is *[Sach Khand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sach_Khand)* (*The Realm of Truth*): the final union of the spirit with God.[^cite_note-p254-39]

### Service and action

Further information: [Seva (Indian religions) § Seva in Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seva_\(Indian_religions\)#Seva_in_Sikhism)

The Sikh gurus taught that by constantly remembering the divine name (*[naam simran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simran)*) and through selfless service (*[sēvā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seva_\(Indian_religions\))*) the devotee overcomes egotism (*[Haumai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haumai)*). This, it states, is the primary root of five evil impulses and the cycle of birth and death.[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89]

Service in Sikhism takes three forms: *Tan* (physical service, i.e. labour), *Man* (mental service, such as dedicating your heart for service of others), and *Dhan* (material service, including financial support).[^cite_note-90] Sikhism stresses *[kirat karō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirat_kar%C5%8D)*, that is, "honest work". Sikh teachings also stress the concept of sharing, or *[vaṇḍ chakkō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Va%E1%B9%87%E1%B8%8D_chakk%C5%8D)*, giving to the needy for the benefit of the community.[^cite_note-91]

### Justice and equality

Sikhism regards God as the true emperor, the king of all kings, the one who dispenses justice through the law of *[karma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma)*, a retributive model and divine grace.[^cite_note-Chanchreek_2007_142-92][^cite_note-Dugga_2001_33-93][^cite_note-Justice-94]

The term for justice in the Sikh tradition is Niāyā.[^cite_note-Justice-94] It is related to the term *dharam*, which in Sikhism connotes 'moral order' and righteousness (derived, but distinct, from the etymologically related Hindu concept of [dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma)).[^cite_note-Justice-94] According to the tenth Sikh guru, [Guru Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh), states Pashaura Singh (a professor of Sikh studies), "one must first try all the peaceful means of negotiation in the pursuit of justice" and if these fail then it is legitimate to "draw the sword in defense of righteousness".[^cite_note-95] Sikhism considers "an attack on dharam is an attack on justice, on righteousness, and on the moral order generally" and the dharam "must be defended at all costs".[^cite_note-Mcleod-96] The divine name is its antidote for pain and vices. Forgiveness is taught as a virtue in Sikhism, yet it also teaches its faithful to shun those with evil intentions and to pick up the sword to fight injustice and religious persecution.[^cite_note-97]

Sikhism does not differentiate religious obligations by sex. God in Sikhism has no sex, and the Sikh scripture does not discriminate against women, nor bar them from any roles.[^cite_note-McLeod2009p70-98] Women in Sikhism have been in positions of leadership, including leading in wars and issuing orders or [hukamnamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hukamnama).[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-McLeod2009p70-98][^cite_note-100]

### Ten Gurus and authority

Main article: [Sikh gurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_gurus)



A rare [Tanjore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanjore_painting)-style painting from the late 19th century depicting the ten Sikh Gurus with [Bhai Bala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhai_Bala) and [Bhai Mardana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhai_Mardana)

The term "[guru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru)" is derived from the [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) *gurū*, meaning teacher, enlightener, guide, or mentor. The traditions and philosophy of Sikhism were established by ten gurus from 1469 to 1708.[^cite_note-Panjab_Publishers-101][^cite_note-Nesbitt2005_a-102] Each guru added to and reinforced the message taught by the previous, resulting in the creation of the Sikh religion. Guru Nanak was the first guru and appointed a disciple as successor. [Guru Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh) was the final guru in human form. Before his death, Guru Gobind Singh decreed in 1708, that the Gurū Granth Sāhib would be the final and perpetual guru of the Sikhs.[^cite_note-granthfinalguru-103]

Guru Nanak stated that his guru is God who is the same from the beginning of time to the end of time.[^cite_note-104] Nanak said to be a God's slave and servant, but maintained that he was only a guide and teacher.[^cite_note-ksingh116-105][^cite_note-106] Nanak stated that the human guru is mortal, who is to be respected and loved but not worshipped.[^cite_note-ksingh116-105] When *guru*, or [satguru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satguru) (lit. 'the true guru') is used in *Gurbani* it is often referring to the highest expression of truthfulness.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109]

[Guru Angad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Angad) succeeded Guru Nanak. Later, an important phase in the development of Sikhism came with the third successor, [Guru Amar Das](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Amar_Das). Guru Nanak's teachings emphasised the pursuit of salvation; Guru Amar Das began building a cohesive community of followers with initiatives such as sanctioning distinctive ceremonies for birth, marriage, and death. Amar Das also established the *manji* (comparable to a [diocese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese)) system of clerical supervision.[^cite_note-p254-39]



The interior of the [Akal Takht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akal_Takht)

The Sikh gurus established a mechanism which allowed the Sikh religion to react as a community to changing circumstances. The sixth guru, [Guru Hargobind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Har_Gobind), was responsible for the creation of the concept of [Akal Takht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akal_Takht) (*throne of the timeless one*), which serves as the supreme decision-making centre of Sikhism and sits opposite the [Harmandir Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmandir_Sahib). The Akal Takht is located in the city of Amritsar. The leader is appointed by the Shiromani Gurdwara Pabandhak Committee (SGPC). The *[Sarbat Ḵẖālsā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbat_Khalsa)* (a representative portion of the Khalsa Panth) historically gathers at the Akal Takht on special festivals such as [Vaisakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi) or [Hola Mohalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hola_Mohalla) and when there is a need to discuss matters that affect the entire Sikh nation. A *[gurmatā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmata)* (literally, 'guru's intention') is an order passed by the Sarbat Ḵẖālsā in the presence of the Gurū Granth Sāhib. A *gurmatā* may only be passed on a subject that affects the fundamental principles of Sikh religion; it is binding upon all Sikhs.[^cite_note-110]

Graph showing Life Spans and Guruship Spans of Sikh Gurus

Approximate Life Spans and Guruship Spans of the 10 Sikh Gurus

The word *guru* in Sikhism also refers to *Akal Purkh* (God), and God and *guru* can sometimes be synonymous in *[Gurbani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurbani)* (Sikh writings).[^cite_note-Panjab_Publishers-101][^cite_note-111]

## Scripture

There is one primary scripture for the Sikhs: the *[Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib)*. It is sometimes synonymously referred to as the *Ādi Granth*.[^cite_note-shacklexvii-112] Chronologically, however, the *Ādi Granth* – literally, 'First Volume' – refers to the version of the scripture created by [Guru Arjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan) in 1604.[^cite_note-113] The Guru Granth Sahib is the final expanded version of the scripture compiled by [Guru Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh).[^cite_note-shacklexvii-112][^cite_note-114] While the Guru Granth Sahib is an unquestioned scripture in Sikhism, another important religious text, the *[Dasam Granth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dasam_Granth)*, does not enjoy universal consensus, but is considered a secondary scripture by many Sikhs.[^cite_note-shacklexvii-112]

### Ādi Granth

Main article: [Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib)

The *Ādi Granth* was compiled primarily by [Bhai Gurdas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhai_Gurdas) under the supervision of [Guru Arjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan) between the years 1603 and 1604.[^cite_note-115] It is written in the [Gurmukhī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmukhi) script, which is a descendant of the [Laṇḍā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La%E1%B9%87%E1%B8%8D%C4%81) script used in the Punjab at that time.[^cite_note-116] The Gurmukhī script was standardised by Guru Angad, the second guru of the Sikhs, for use in the Sikh scriptures and is thought to have been influenced by the [Śāradā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9A%C4%81rad%C4%81_script) and [Devanāgarī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devan%C4%81gar%C4%AB) scripts. An authoritative scripture was created to protect the integrity of hymns and teachings of the Sikh gurus, as well as thirteen Hindu and two Muslim bhagats of the [Bhakti movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti_movement) sant tradition in medieval India.[^cite_note-117] The thirteen Hindu *bhagats* whose teachings were entered into the text included [Ramananda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramananda), [Namdev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namdev), [Pipa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Pipa), [Ravidas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravidas), [Beni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Beni), [Bhikhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Bhikhan), [Dhanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Dhanna), [Jaidev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayadeva), [Parmanand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Parmanand), [Sadhana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Sadhana), [Sain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Sain), [Sur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sur_\(poet\)), [Trilochan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Trilochan), while the two Muslim *bhagats* were [Kabir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabir) and Sufi saint [Farid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fariduddin_Ganjshakar).[^cite_note-shapiro924-118][^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121] However, the bhagats in context often spoke of transcending their religious labels; Kabir, often attributed to being a Muslim, states in the *Ādi Granth*, "I am not Hindu nor Muslim."[^cite_note-122] The gurus following this message taught that different methods of devotion are for the same infinite God.[^cite_note-123] The *Ādi Granth* is an early draft of the Guru Granth Sahib.

### Guru Granth Sahib



Gurū Granth Sāhib – the primary scripture of Sikhism

Main article: [Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib)

The Guru Granth Sahib is the holy scripture of the Sikhs and is regarded as the living guru.

#### Compilation

The Guru Granth Sahib started as a volume of Guru Nanak's poetic compositions. Prior to his death, he passed on his volume to the subsequent guru, Guru Angad. The final version of the Guru Granth Sahib was compiled by [Guru Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh) in 1678. It consists of the original Ādi Granth with the addition of [Guru Tegh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Tegh_Bahadur)'s hymns. The predominant bulk of Guru Granth Sahib consists of compositions by seven Sikh gurus: Guru Nanak, Guru Angad, Guru Amar Das, Guru Ram Das, Guru Arjan, Guru Teg Bahadur and Guru Gobind Singh. It also contains the traditions and teachings of thirteen [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) Bhakti movement *sants* (saints) such as [Ramananda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramananda), [Namdev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namdev), [Sant kabir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sant_kabir?action=edit\&redlink=1) among others, and two [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) saints: the Sufi [Sheikh Farid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Farid).[^cite_note-p254-39][^cite_note-shapiro924-118]

The text comprises 6,000 *[śabads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabhad)* (line compositions),[^cite_note-shacklexvii-112] which are poetically rendered and set to rhythmic ancient north Indian classical music.[^cite_note-king359-124] The bulk of the scripture is classified into sixty *[rāgas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raga)*, with each Granth rāga subdivided according to length and author. The hymns in the scripture are arranged primarily by the *rāgas* in which they are read.[^cite_note-shacklexvii-112]

#### Language and script



[Mul Mantar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mul_Mantar) written by [Guru Har Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Har_Rai), showing the Ik Onkar at top

The primary language used in the scripture is known as *[Sant Bhāṣā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sant_Bhasha)*, a language related to both [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language) and [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi_language) and used extensively across medieval northern India by proponents of popular devotional religion ([bhakti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti)).[^cite_note-parrinderp259-125] The text is printed in [Gurumukhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmukhi) script, believed to have been developed by Guru Angad.[^cite_note-shacklexvii-112] The language shares the Indo-European roots found in numerous regional languages of India.[^cite_note-126]

#### Teachings



A group of [Sikh musicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_music) called Dhadi at the [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple) complex

The vision in the Guru Granth Sahib, states Torkel Brekke, is a society based on divine justice without oppression.[^cite_note-brekke673-127]

One God exists, truth by name, creative power, without fear, without enmity, timeless form, unborn, self-existent, by the guru's grace.

[^cite_note-128]

([Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language): ੴ ਸਤਿ ਨਾਮੁ ਕਰਤਾ ਪੁਰਖੁ ਨਿਰਭਉ ਨਿਰਵੈਰੁ ਅਕਾਲ ਮੂਰਤਿ ਅਜੂਨੀ ਸੈਭੰ ਗੁਰ ਪ੍ਰਸਾਦਿ ॥, romanised: *Ika ōaṅkāra sati nāmu karatā purakhu nirabha'u niravairu akāla mūrati ajūnī saibhaṅ gura prasādi*)

#### As guru

The tenth guru, Guru Gobind Singh, named the Sikh scripture Guru Granth Sahib as his successor, terminating the line of human gurus and making the scripture the literal embodiment of the eternal, impersonal guru, serving as the spiritual guide for Sikhs.[^cite_note-WH_McLeod_2014_page_17-129][^cite_note-William_James_2011_pages_241-242-130][^cite_note-granthfinalguru-103][^cite_note-131]

The Guru Granth Sahib is installed in all Sikh *Gurdwara*s (temples); many Sikhs bow or prostrate before it when entering the temple. The Guru Granth Sahib is installed every morning and put to bed at night in many *Gurdwaras*.[^cite_note-132] The Granth is revered as eternal *[gurbānī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurbani)* and the spiritual authority.[^cite_note-133]

The copies of the Guru Granth Sahib are not regarded as material objects but as living subjects. According to Myrvold, the Sikh scripture is treated with respect like a living person, in a manner similar to the Gospel in early Christian worship. Old copies of the Sikh scripture are not thrown away. Instead, funerary services are performed.[^cite_note-Myrvold-134]

In India, the Guru Granth Sahib is officially recognised by the Supreme Court of India as a judicial person who can receive donations and own land. Yet, some Sikhs also warn that, without proper comprehension of the text, veneration for the text can lead to [bibliolatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliolatry), with the concrete form of the teachings becoming the object of worship instead of the teachings themselves.[^cite_note-Myrvold-134]

#### Distinction from other monotheistic religions

The Abrahamic religions do not deny the existence of spiritual beings such as angels, Satan (Iblis), and jinn under the one true God. However, Sikhism does not acknowledge the existence of such spiritual entities; it recognises only the one, formless, omnipotent, and omniscient God (Waheguru), emphasising the directness and oneness of God. Although Sikh scriptures mention angels, devas, Yama, and demons, these references are merely literary metaphors or borrowings, and are not regarded as descriptions of real, existing spiritual beings.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136]

#### Relation to Hinduism and Islam

Main articles: [Sikhism and Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_and_Hinduism) and [Islam and Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_and_Sikhism)

The Sikh scriptures use [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) terminology, with references to the [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas), and the names of gods and goddesses in Hindu bhakti movement traditions, such as Vishnu, Shiva, Brahma, Parvati, Lakshmi, Saraswati, Rama, Krishna, but not to worship.[^cite_note-brekke673-127][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138] It also refers to the spiritual concepts in Hinduism (*[Ishvara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishvara), [Bhagavan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagavan), [Brahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahman)*) and the idea of God in [Islam](6037917.md) (*[Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allah)*) to assert that these are just "alternate names for the Almighty One".[^cite_note-139]

While the Guru Granth Sahib acknowledges the [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas), [Puranas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puranas) and [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran),[^cite_note-140] it does not imply a [syncretic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretic_religion) bridge between Hinduism and Islam,[^cite_note-141] but emphasises focusing on [nitnem banis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitnem) like [Japu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japji_Sahib) (repeating [mantra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantra) of the divine Name of God – [Waheguru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waheguru)), instead of practices such as praying by prostrating on the ground to God towards a [specific direction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibla) by Muslims, or Hindu rituals such as wearing thread; the former being, though, a disciplinary aspect of worship, given [Dhikr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhikr) (remembrance of Allah) is similarly emphasised in Islam.[^cite_note-142]

### Dasam Granth



The Dasam Granth is a Sikh scripture which contains texts attributed to Guru Gobind Singh, including his autobiography [Bachittar Natak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachittar_Natak). The major narrative in the text is on [Chaubis Avtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaubis_Avtar) (24 [Avatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avatars) of Hindu god [Vishnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vishnu)), [Rudra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudra), [Brahma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahma), the Hindu warrior goddess [Chandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandi) and a story of [Rama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rama) in [Bachittar Natak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachittar_Natak).

[^cite_note-jdeol31-143]

Main article: [Dasam Granth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dasam_Granth)

The Dasam Granth is a Sikh scripture which contains texts attributed to Guru Gobind Singh. Scholars, on the other hand, attribute the work to after the guru's death, being authored by an unknown poet. The *Dasam Granth* is important to a great number of Sikhs. However, it does not have the same authority as the *Guru Granth Sahib*. Some compositions of the *Dasam Granth* like [Jaap Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaap_Sahib) ([Amrit Savaiye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amrit_Savaiye)), and [Benti Chaupai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaupai_\(Sikhism\)) are part of the daily prayers ([Nitnem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitnem)) for Sikhs.[^cite_note-144] The first verse of the [ardās](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ard%C4%81s) prayer is from [Chandi di Var](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandi_di_Var). The *Dasam Granth* is largely a version of Hindu mythology from the [Puranas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puranas): secular stories from a variety of sources called *Charitro Pakhyan*—tales to protect careless men from the perils of lust.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

Many versions of *Dasam Granth* exist, and the authenticity of the *Dasam Granth* has, in modern times, become one of the most debated topics within Sikhism. The [Akali Nihangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihang) consider the Dasam and [Sarbloh Granth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbloh_Granth) as extensions of the [Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib).[^cite_note-147] The text played a significant role in Sikh history, but in modern times parts of the text have seen antipathy and discussion among Sikhs.[^cite_note-jdeol31-143]

### Sarbloh Granth

The [Sarbloh Granth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarbloh_Granth) is a holy text containing 6,500 poetic stanzas traditionally attributed to Guru Gobind Singh. Scholars, on the other hand, attribute the work to after the guru's death, being authored by an unknown poet.[^cite_note-148] This scripture contains, alongside various topics, the [Sikh Art and Laws of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharamyudh_\(Sikhism\)). Akali Nihangs largely revere this scripture, and many non-Nihang Sikhs reject it as an authentic work of the 10th guru.[^cite_note-149] According to [Harbans Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harbans_Singh) the authenticity of the work is rejected on the grounds of its writing style and mastery of poetry not matching up with Guru Gobind Singh's *Dasam Granth* work. Also, the text makes mention of a work composed in 1719, much after the death of Guru Gobind Singh.[^cite_note-150] [W. H. McLeod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._H._McLeod) dates the work to the late 18th century and believes an unknown poet authored it, which was mistakenly attributed to the tenth guru.[^cite_note-151]

### Janamsakhis

Main article: [Janamsakhis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janamsakhis)

The Janamsākhīs (literally *birth stories*) are writings that profess to be biographies of Guru Nanak. Although not scripture in the strictest sense, they provide a [hagiographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagiography) look at Guru Nanak's life and the early start of Sikhism.

## Observances

See also: [Sikh rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_rites) and [Sikh culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_culture)



The [Darbar Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darbar_Sahib_Hall) of a [Gurdwara](482849.md)

Observant Sikhs adhere to long-standing practices and traditions to strengthen and express their faith. The daily recitation of the divine name of God, Waheguru, and from a memory of specific passages from the Guru Granth Sahib, like the *Japu* (or *Japjī*, literally *chant*) hymns is recommended immediately after rising and bathing. Baptised Sikhs recite the five-morning prayers, the evening and night prayer. Family customs include both reading passages from the scripture and attending the [gurdwara](482849.md) (also *gurduārā*, meaning *the doorway to God*; sometimes transliterated as *Gurudwara*). There are many gurdwaras prominently constructed and maintained across India, as well as in almost every country where Sikhs reside. Gurdwaras are open to all, regardless of religion, background, caste, or race.[^cite_note-brittanica-gurdwara-152]

Worship in a gurdwara consists chiefly of the singing of passages from the scripture. Sikhs will commonly prostrate before the holy scripture when entering a gurdwara. The recitation of the eighteenth century *[ardās](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ard%C4%81s)* is also customary for attending Sikhs. The ardās recalls past sufferings and glories of the community, invoking divine grace for all humanity.[^cite_note-p260-153]

The gurdwara is also the location for the historic Sikh practice of *[langar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langar_\(Sikhism\))* or the community meal. All gurdwaras are open to anyone of any faith for a free meal, which is always vegetarian.[^cite_note-154] People eat together, and the kitchen is maintained and serviced by Sikh community volunteers.[^cite_note-155]

### Sikh festivals/events

[Guru Amar Das](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Amar_Das) chose festivals for celebration by Sikhs like [Vaisakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi), wherein he asked Sikhs to assemble and share the festivities as a community.[^cite_note-ColeSambhi1995p135-156][^cite_note-157]

Vaisakhi is one of the most important festivals of Sikhs, while other significant festivals commemorate the birth, lives of the gurus and Sikh martyrs. Historically, these festivals have been based on the moon calendar [Bikrami calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bikrami_calendar).[^cite_note-nesbittbc-158] In 2003, the [SGPC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SGPC), the Sikh organisation in charge of upkeep of the historical gurdwaras of Punjab, adopted [Nanakshahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanakshahi_calendar) calendar.[^cite_note-nesbittbc-158] The new calendar is highly controversial among Sikhs and is not universally accepted. Sikh festivals include the following:



[Nagar Kirtan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagar_Kirtan) crowd listening to [Kirtan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirtan) at [Yuba City, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuba_City,_California)

- Band Chor Diwas has been another important Sikh festival in its history.

  [^cite_note-159]

  &#x20;In recent years, instead of Diwali, the post-2003 calendar released by SGPC has named it the [Bandi Chhor Divas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandi_Chhor_Divas).

  [^cite_note-160]

- [Hola Mohalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hola_Mohalla) is a tradition started by Guru Gobind Singh. It starts the day after Sikhs celebrate [Holi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holi),

  [^cite_note-McLeod2009p95-161]

  &#x20;sometimes referred to as *Hola*.

  [^cite_note-Roy2005p192-162]

  &#x20;Guru Gobind Singh modified Holi with a three-day Hola Mohalla extension festival of martial arts. The extension started the day after the Holi festival in [Anandpur Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anandpur_Sahib), where Sikh soldiers would train in mock battles, compete in horsemanship, athletics, archery and military exercises.

  [^cite_note-163]

  [^cite_note-164]

### Ceremonies and customs



[Sikh wedding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_wedding)



Sikh funeral procession, [Mandi, Himachal Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandi,_Himachal_Pradesh)

Sikhs have also supported and helped develop major pilgrimage traditions to sacred sites such as Harmandir Sahib, Anandpur Sahib, Fatehgarh Sahib, Patna Sahib, Hazur Nanded Sahib, Hemkund Sahib and others.[^cite_note-Oberoi1994p68-165] Sikh pilgrims and Sikhs of other sects customarily consider these as holy and a part of their *Tirath*.[^cite_note-Ferrari2011p48-166] The [Hola Mohalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hola_Mohalla) around the festival of [Holi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holi), for example, is a ceremonial and customary gathering every year in [Anandpur Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anandpur_Sahib) attracting over 100,000 Sikhs.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] Major Sikh temples feature a *[sarovar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarovar)* where some Sikhs take a customary dip. Some take home the [sacred water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_waters) of the tank particularly for sick friends and relatives,[^cite_note-singha7-169][^cite_note-170] believing that the waters of such sacred sites have restorative powers and the ability to purify one's *karma*.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-singha7-169] The various gurus of Sikhism have had different approaches to pilgrimage.[^cite_note-173]

Upon a child's birth, the Guru Granth Sahib is opened at a random point and the child is named using the first letter on the top left hand corner of the left page. All boys are given the last name [Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singh), and all girls are given the last name [Kaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaur) (this was once a title which was conferred on an individual upon joining the [Khalsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa)).[^cite_note-174]

The Sikh marriage ritual includes the *[anand kāraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anand_Karaj)* ceremony.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-Ruether2006p700-176] The marriage ceremony is performed in front of the Guru Granth Sahib by a baptised Khalsa, Granthi of the Gurdwara.[^cite_note-HaarKalsi2009p10-177][^cite_note-fenech33-178] Its official recognition and adoption came in 1909, during the [Singh Sabha Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singh_Sabha_Movement).[^cite_note-fenech33-178]

Upon death, the body of a Sikh is usually cremated. If this is not possible, any respectful means of disposing the body may be employed. The *kīrtan sōhilā* and *ardās* prayers are performed during the funeral ceremony (known as *[antim sanskār](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antam_Sanskar)*).[^cite_note-179]

### Initiation and the Khalsa

[Khalsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa) (meaning "pure and sovereign") is the collective name given by Guru Gobind Singh,[^cite_note-pashauraarjan-180][^cite_note-Gandhi-181] to those Sikhs who have been fully initiated by taking part in a ceremony called *[ammrit sañcār](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammrit_sa%C3%B1c%C4%81r)* (nectar ceremony).[^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p23-182] During this ceremony, sweetened water is stirred with a double-edged sword while liturgical prayers are sung; it is offered to the initiating Sikh, who ritually drinks it.[^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p23-182] Many Sikhs are not formally and fully initiated, as they do not undergo this ceremony, but do adhere to some components of Sikhism and identify as Sikhs. The initiated Sikh, who is believed to be [reborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dvija#The_meaning_of_the_two_births), is referred to as [Amritdhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amritdhari) or Khalsa Sikh, while those who are not initiated or baptised are referred to as Kesdhari or Sahajdhari Sikhs.[^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p23-182][^cite_note-183]

The first time that this ceremony took place was on [Vaisakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi), which fell on 30 March 1699 at [Anandpur Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anandpur_Sahib) in Punjab.[^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p23-182] It was on that occasion that Gobind Singh baptised the [Pañj Piārē](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panj_Piare) – the five beloved ones, who in turn baptised Guru Gobind Singh himself. To males who initiated, the last name Singh, meaning "lion", was given, while the last name Kaur, meaning "princess", was given to baptised Sikh females.[^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p23-182]

Baptised [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs) wear five items, called the [five Ks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Ks) (in Punjabi known as *pañj kakkē* or *pañj kakār*), at all times. The five items are: *[kēs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kesh_\(Sikhism\))* (uncut hair), *[kaṅghā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanga_\(Sikhism\))* (small wooden comb), *[kaṛā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara_\(Sikhism\))* (circular steel or iron bracelet), *[kirpān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirpan)* (sword/dagger), and *[kacchera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaccha)* (special undergarment).[^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p23-182] The five Ks have both practical and symbolic purposes.[^cite_note-184]

## History

Main article: [History of Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Sikhism)



Map of Punjab, where Sikhism originated, against present-day borders

Sikhism originated around the 15th century.[^cite_note-Nesbitt2005-185] Guru Nanak (1469–1539), the founder of Sikhism, was born in the village of *Rāi Bhōi dī Talwandī*, now called [Nankana Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nankana_Sahib) (in present-day Pakistan).[^cite_note-186] His parents were [Punjabi Khatri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_Khatri) Hindus.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] According to the [hagiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagiography) *Puratan Janamsakhi* composed more than two centuries after his death and probably based on [oral tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition),[^cite_note-189] Nanak as a boy was fascinated by religion and spiritual matters, spending time with wandering ascetics and holy men.[^cite_note-shacklexiii-190] His friend was Mardana, a Muslim. Together they would sing devotional songs all night in front of the public, and bathe in the river in the morning. One day, at the usual bath, Nanak went missing and his family feared he had drowned. Three days later he returned home, and declared: "There is no [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu), there is no Muslim" ("*nā kōi hindū nā kōi musalmān*"). Thereafter, Nanak started preaching his ideas that form the tenets of Sikhism. In 1526, Guru Nanak at age 50, started a small commune in Kartarpur and his disciples came to be known as *Sikhs*.[^cite_note-shacklexiii-190] Although the exact account of his itinerary is disputed, hagiographic accounts state he made five major journeys, spanning thousands of miles: the first tour being east towards [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal) and [Assam](186162.md); the second south towards [Andhra](2377.md) and [Tamil Nadu](29918.md); the third north to [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir), [Ladakh](303611.md), and [Mount Sumeru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Meru)[^cite_note-191] in [Tibet](31516.md); and the fourth to [Baghdad](4492.md).[^cite_note-192] In his last and final tour, he returned to the banks of the Ravi River to end his days.[^cite_note-193]

There are two competing theories on Guru Nanak's teachings.[^cite_note-arvindmandair131-194] One, according to Cole and Sambhi, is based on hagiographical [Janamsakhis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janamsakhis),[^cite_note-195] and states that Nanak's teachings and Sikhism were a revelation from God, and not a social protest movement nor any attempt to reconcile Hinduism and Islam in the 15th century.[^cite_note-Cole_Sambhi_Dic-196] The other states that Nanak was a [guru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru). According to Singha, "Sikhism does not subscribe to the theory of incarnation or the concept of prophethood. But it has a pivotal concept of Guru. He is not an incarnation of God, not even a prophet. He is an illumined soul."[^cite_note-HS_Singha_2009_page_104-197] The second theory continues that hagiographical *Janamsakhis* were not written by Nanak, but by later followers without regard for historical accuracy, and contain numerous legends and myths created to show respect for Nanak.[^cite_note-198] The term *revelation*, clarify Cole and Sambhi, in Sikhism is not limited to the teachings of Nanak, but is extended to all Sikh gurus, as well as the words of past, present and future men and women, who possess divine knowledge intuitively through meditation. The Sikh revelations include the words of non-Sikh [bhagats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat), some who lived and died before the birth of Nanak, and whose teachings are part of the Sikh scriptures.[^cite_note-199] The Adi Granth and successive Sikh gurus repeatedly emphasised, states Mandair, that Sikhism is "not about hearing voices from God, but it is about changing the nature of the human mind, and anyone can achieve direct experience and spiritual perfection at any time".[^cite_note-arvindmandair131-194]

### Historical influences

Sikhism arose in predominantly an [Indic milieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_religions) with some [Islamic](6037917.md) influence,[^cite_note-200] with it being especially being influenced by the [Sant tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sant_Mat).[^cite_note-201] The roots of the Sikh tradition are, states [Louis Fenech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_E._Fenech), perhaps in the [Sant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sant_\(religion\))-tradition of India whose ideology grew to become the Bhakti tradition.[^cite_note-Louis_Fenech_2014_page_35-202] Furthermore, adds Fenech:[^cite_note-203]

> Few Sikhs would mention these Indic texts and ideologies in the same breadth as the Sikh tradition, let alone trace elements of their tradition to this chronological and ideological point, *despite the fact* that the Indic mythology permeates the Sikh sacred canon, the *Guru Granth Sahib*, and the secondary canon, the *Dasam Granth* ... and adds delicate nuance and substance to the sacred symbolic universe of the Sikhs of today and of their past ancestors.

The development of Sikhism was influenced by the [Bhakti movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti_movement);[^cite_note-David_Lorenzen_1995_pages_1-2-204][^cite_note-Louis_Fenech_2014_page_35-202][^cite_note-encyclobritannicasikh-205][^cite_note-Kitagawa2013-206] however, Sikhism was not simply an extension of the Bhakti movement.[^cite_note-Singha-70][^cite_note-Pruthi-207] Sikhism, for instance, disagreed with some of the views of Bhakti saints Kabir and Ravidas.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-Pruthi-207] Other historical influences on the development of Sikhism were [Sant Mat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sant_Mat), [Vaishnavism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaishnavism) (especially via the Bhakti movement), and [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism). However, Sikhism differs from Vaishnavist theology in regards to [idolatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry_in_Sikhism), incarnation, the Sikhs' heavy emphasis on inward devotion. Sikhs hold that their religion was directly inspired by God and some take offence at the claim that it is a [syncretic religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_syncretism) incorporating Hinduism and Islam.[^cite_note-209] Scholar [James R. Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_R._Lewis_\(scholar\)) has criticized this syncretist approach and understanding of the religion.[^cite_note-210] Sikhism developed while the region was being ruled by the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire). Two of the Sikh gurus, [Guru Arjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan) and [Guru Tegh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Tegh_Bahadur), refused to convert to Islam and were tortured and executed by the Mughal rulers.[^cite_note-pashauraarjan-180][^cite_note-211] The Islamic era persecution of Sikhs triggered the founding of the [Khalsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa), as an order for freedom of conscience and religion.[^cite_note-pashauraarjan-180][^cite_note-212][^cite_note-Gandhi-181] A Sikh is expected to embody the qualities of a "Sant-Sipāhī" –  a [saint-soldier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sant_Sipahi).[^cite_note-Chanchreek_2007_142-92][^cite_note-Dugga_2001_33-93]

### Growth of Sikhism



[Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak) explaining [Sikh teachings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_beliefs) to [Sadhus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadhu)

After its inception, Sikhism grew as it gained converts among Hindus and Muslims in the Punjab region.[^cite_note-Singh2008-213][^cite_note-Singh1989-214][^cite_note-215][^cite_note-Singh1996-216] In 1539, Guru Nanak chose his disciple Lahiṇā as a successor to the Guruship rather than either of his sons. Lahiṇā was named [Guru Angad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Angad) and became the second guru of the Sikhs.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-Fenech2014p36-218] Nanak conferred his choice at the town of [Kartarpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartarpur_\(Pakistan\)) on the banks of the river [Ravi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravi_River). [Sri Chand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Chand), Guru Nanak's son was also a religious man, and continued his own commune of Sikhs. His followers came to be known as the [Udasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Udasi) Sikhs, the first parallel sect of Sikhism that formed in [Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh) history.[^cite_note-Oberoi1994p78-219] The Udasis believe that the Guruship should have gone to Sri Chand, since he was a man of pious habits in addition to being Nanak's son.[^cite_note-Oberoi1994p78-219]

Guru Angad, before joining Guru Nanak's commune, worked as a *[pujari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pujari)* (priest) and religious teacher centred around Hindu goddess [Durga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durga).[^cite_note-Fenech2014p36-218][^cite_note-ColeSambhi1995p18-220] On Nanak's advice, Guru Angad moved from Kartarpur to Khadur, where his wife [Khivi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mata_Khivi) and children were living, until he was able to bridge the divide between his followers and the Udasis. Guru Angad continued the work started by Guru Nanak and is widely credited for standardising the [Gurmukhī script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmukh%C4%AB_script) as used in the sacred scripture of the Sikhs.[^cite_note-ColeSambhi1995p18-220]

[Guru Amar Das](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Amar_Das) became the third Sikh guru in 1552 at the age of 73. He adhered to the [Vaishnavism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaishnavism) tradition of Hinduism for much of his life, before joining the commune of Guru Angad.[^cite_note-FenechMcLeod2014p29-221][^cite_note-222] [Goindval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goindval) became an important centre for Sikhism during the Guruship of Guru Amar Das. He was a reformer, and discouraged veiling of women's faces (a Muslim custom) as well as [sati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sati_\(practice\)) (a Hindu custom).[^cite_note-eosamardas-223][^cite_note-224] He encouraged the [Kshatriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kshatriya) people to fight in order to protect people and for the sake of justice, stating this is [Dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma).[^cite_note-Sambhi2005p29-225] Guru Amar Das started the tradition of appointing [*manji*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manji_\(Sikhism\)) (zones of religious administration with an appointed chief called *sangatias*),[^cite_note-FenechMcLeod2014p29-221] introduced the *[dasvandh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dasvandh)* (tithe) system of revenue collection in the name of guru and as pooled community religious resource,[^cite_note-Farhadian2015p342-226] and the famed *[langar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langar_\(Sikhism\))* tradition of Sikhism where anyone, without discrimination of any kind, could get a free meal in a communal seating. The collection of revenue from Sikhs through regional appointees helped Sikhism grow.[^cite_note-FenechMcLeod2014p29-221][^cite_note-HaarKalsi2009p21-227]

Guru Amar Das named his disciple and son-in-law Jēṭhā as the next guru, who came to be known as [Guru Ram Das](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Ram_Das). The new guru faced hostilities from the sons of Guru Amar Das and therefore shifted his official base to lands identified by Guru Amar Das as Guru-ka-Chak.[^cite_note-Mandair2013p38-228] He moved his commune of Sikhs there and the place then was called Ramdaspur, after him. This city grew and later became [Amritsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amritsar) – the holiest city of Sikhism.[^cite_note-McLeod1990p28-229] Guru Ram Das expanded the *manji* organisation for clerical appointments in Sikh temples, and for revenue collections to theologically and economically support the Sikh movement.[^cite_note-Mandair2013p38-228]

In 1581, [Guru Arjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan), the youngest son of Guru Ram Das, became the fifth guru of the Sikhs. The choice of successor, as throughout most of the history of Sikh guru successions, led to disputes and internal divisions among the Sikhs.[^cite_note-ShackleMandair2013xv-230] The elder son of Guru Ram Das named [Prithi Chand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prithi_Chand) is remembered in the Sikh tradition as vehemently opposing Guru Arjan, creating a faction Sikh community which the Sikhs following Guru Arjan called as *Minaas* (literally, "scoundrels").[^cite_note-fenech39-231][^cite_note-McLeod2009p20-232]

Guru Arjan is remembered among Sikhs for many accomplishments. He built the first [Harimandir Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harimandir_Sahib) (later to become the [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple)). He was a poet, and he created the first edition of Sikh sacred text known as the [Ādi Granth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adi_Granth) (literally "the first book") and included the writings of the first five gurus and other enlightened 13 Hindu and 2 Muslim Sufi saints. In 1606, he was tortured and killed by the [Mughal emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_emperors) [Jahangir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahangir),[^cite_note-233] for refusing to convert to Islam.[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-pashauraarjan-180][^cite_note-235] His martyrdom is considered a watershed event in the history of Sikhism.[^cite_note-pashauraarjan-180][^cite_note-236]

### Political advancement

After the martyrdom of Guru Arjan, his son [Guru Hargobind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Hargobind) at age eleven became the sixth guru of the Sikhs, and Sikhism dramatically evolved to become a political movement in addition to being religious.[^cite_note-pashaura29-237] Guru Hargobind carried two swords, calling one spiritual and the other for temporal purpose, reflecting the concept of Miri Piri.[^cite_note-238] According to the Sikh tradition, Guru Arjan asked his son Hargobind to start a military tradition to protect the [Sikh people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_people) and always keep himself surrounded by armed Sikhs. The building of an armed Sikh militia began with Guru Hargobind.[^cite_note-pashaura29-237] Guru Hargobind was soon arrested by the Mughals and kept in jail in Gwalior. It is unclear how many years he served in prison, with different texts stating it to be between 2 and 12.[^cite_note-mandair48-239] He married three women, built a fort to defend Ramdaspur and created a formal court called [Akal Takht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akal_Takht), now the highest Khalsa Sikh religious authority.[^cite_note-Phyllis2004-240]

In 1644, Guru Hargobind named his grandson [Har Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Har_Rai) as the guru. The Mughal emperor [Shah Jahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Jahan) attempted political means to undermine the Sikh tradition, by dividing and influencing the succession.[^cite_note-mandair49-241] The Mughal ruler gave land grants to Dhir Mal, a grandson of Guru Hargobind living in Kartarpur, and attempted to encourage Sikhs to recognise Dhir Mal as the rightful successor to Guru Hargobind.[^cite_note-mandair49-241] Dhir Mal issued statements in favour of the Mughal state and critical of his grandfather [Guru Arjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan). Guru Hargobind rejected Dhir Mal, the latter refused to give up the original version of the Adi Granth he had, and the Sikh community was divided.[^cite_note-mandair49-241]

Guru Har Rai is famed to have met Dara Shikoh during a time Dara Shikoh and his younger brother Aurangzeb were in a bitter succession fight. Aurangzeb summoned Guru Har Rai, who refused to go and sent his elder son Ram Rai instead.[^cite_note-McLeod2014p260-242] The emperor found a verse in the Sikh scripture insulting to Muslims, and Ram Rai agreed it was a mistake then changed it. Ram Rai thus pleased Aurangzeb, but displeased Guru Har Rai who excommunicated his elder son. He nominated his younger son [Guru Har Krishan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Har_Krishan) to succeed him in 1661. Aurangzeb responded by granting Ram Rai a [jagir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagir) (land grant). Ram Rai founded a town there and enjoyed Aurangzeb's patronage; the town came to be known as Dehradun, after *Dehra* referring to Ram Rai's shrine. [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh) who followed Ram Rai came to be known as [Ramraiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramraiya) Sikhs.[^cite_note-McLeod2014p260-242][^cite_note-243] Guru Har Krishan became the eighth guru at the age of five, and died of smallpox before reaching the age of eight. No hymns composed by these three gurus are included in the Guru Granth Sahib.[^cite_note-244]

[Guru Tegh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Tegh_Bahadur), the uncle of Guru Har Krishan, became guru in 1665. Tegh Bahadur resisted the forced conversions of [Kashmiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir) [Pandits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandit)[^cite_note-245] and non-Muslims[^cite_note-Mandair2013p53-246] to [Islam](6037917.md), and was publicly beheaded in 1675 on the orders of [Mughal emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) [Aurangzeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurangzeb) in [Delhi](37756.md) for refusing to convert to Islam.[^cite_note-cs2013-247][^cite_note-pashauraarjan-180][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251][^cite_note-fenech4-252] His beheading traumatised the Sikhs. His body was cremated in Delhi, while the head was carried secretively by Sikhs and cremated in [Anandpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anandpur_Sahib). He was succeeded by his son, Gobind Rai, who militarised his followers by creating the [Khalsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa) in 1699, and baptising the *[Pañj Piārē](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panj_Piare)*.[^cite_note-MandairShackle2013p25-253] From then on, he was known as [Guru Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh), and Sikh identity was redefined into a political force resisting religious persecution.[^cite_note-ws1981-254]

- Gurudwara Sis Ganj Sahib in Delhi. The long window under the marble platform is the location where Guru Tegh Bahadur was executed by the Mughals.

  [Gurudwara Sis Ganj Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurudwara_Sis_Ganj_Sahib) in Delhi. The long window under the marble platform is the location where [Guru Tegh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Tegh_Bahadur) was executed by the Mughals.
