  Artistic rendering of the execution of [Bhai Mati Das](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhai_Mati_Das) by the Mughals. This image is from a [Sikh Ajaibghar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Ajaibghar) near the towns of Mohali and Sirhind in Punjab, India.&#x20;

#### Sikh confederacy and the rise of the Khalsa

- Sculpture at Mehdiana Sahib of the execution of Banda Singh Bahadur in 1716 by the Mughals

  Sculpture at [Mehdiana Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdiana_Sahib) of the execution of [Banda Singh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banda_Singh_Bahadur) in 1716 by the Mughals

- Some bodyguards of Maharaja Ranjit Singh at the Sikh capital, Lahore, Punjab

  Some bodyguards of [Maharaja Ranjit Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranjit_Singh) at the Sikh capital, Lahore, Punjab

[Guru Gobind Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Gobind_Singh) inaugurated the [Khalsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa) (the collective body of all [initiated Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amrit_Sanskar)) as the Sikh temporal authority in the year 1699. It created a community that combines its spiritual purpose and goals with political and military duties.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-granthfinalguru-103][^cite_note-parrinderp259-125] Shortly before his death, Guru Gobind Singh proclaimed the [Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib) to be the ultimate spiritual authority for the Sikhs.[^cite_note-256]

After the Guru Gobind's death, [Banda Singh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banda_Singh_Bahadur) became the commander-in-chief of the Khalsa.[^cite_note-257] He organised the civilian rebellion and abolished or halted the [Zamindari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zamindar) system in time he was active and gave the farmers [proprietorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-ownership) of their own land.[^cite_note-258]

The Sikh empire, with its capital in [Lahore](125315.md), spread over almost 200,000 square miles (520,000 square kilometres) comprising what is now northwestern [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent). The Sikh Empire entered into a treaty with the colonial British powers, with each side recognising Sutlej River as the line of control and agreeing not to invade the other side.[^cite_note-259] Ranjit Singh's most lasting legacy was the restoration and expansion of the [Harmandir Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmandir_Sahib), most revered [Gurudwara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurudwara) of the Sikhs, with marble and gold, from which the popular name of the "[Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple)" is derived.[^cite_note-260] After the death of Ranjit Singh in 1839, the Sikh Empire fell into disorder. Ranjit Singh had failed to establish a lasting structure for Sikh government or stable succession, and the Sikh Empire rapidly declined after his death. Factions divided the Sikhs, and led to [Anglo-Sikh wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Sikh_War). The British defeated the confused and demoralised [Khalsa forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Khalsa_Army), then disbanded them into destitution.[^cite_note-Oberoi1994p207-261] The youngest son of Ranjit Singh, named [Duleep Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maharaja_Duleep_Singh), ultimately succeeded, but he was arrested and exiled after the defeat of the Sikhs.[^cite_note-262]

#### Singh Sabha movement

Main article: [Singh Sabha Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singh_Sabha_Movement)

The Singh Sabha movement, a movement to revitalise Sikhism, also saw the resurgence of the [Khalsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa) after their defeat in wars with the British[^cite_note-263] – latterly in the [Second Anglo-Sikh War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Sikh_War) – and the subsequent decline and corruption of Sikh institutions during colonial rule, and the proselytisation of other faith groups in the Punjab.[^cite_note-barrierssm-264][^cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica_Online-265] It was started in the 1870s, and after a period of interfactional rivalry, united under the Tat Khalsa to reinvigorate Sikh practice and institutions.[^cite_note-perplexed84-266]

The last Maharaja of the Sikh Empire, Duleep Singh, converted to Christianity in 1853, a controversial but influential event in Sikh history. Along with his conversion, and after Sikh Empire had been dissolved and the region made a part of the colonial British Empire, [proselytising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proselytism) activities of [Christians](18337522.md), [Brahmo Samajis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmo_Samaj), [Arya Samaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arya_Samaj), Muslim Anjuman-i-Islamia and Ahmadiyah sought to convert the Sikhs in northwestern Indian subcontinent into their respective faiths.[^cite_note-barrierssm-264][^cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica_Online-265] These developments launched the Singh Sabha Movement.[^cite_note-barrierssm-264][^cite_note-Encyclopædia_Britannica_Online-265]

The first meeting of the movement was in the [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple), Amritsar in 1873, and it was largely launched by the [Sanatan Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanatan_Sikh), Gianis, priests, and granthis.[^cite_note-267] Shortly thereafter, Nihang Sikhs began influencing the movement, followed by a sustained campaign by the [Tat Khalsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tat_Khalsa), which had quickly gained dominance by the early 1880s.[^cite_note-perplexed84-266][^cite_note-Oberoi1994-268] The movement became a struggle between Sanatan Sikhs and Tat Khalsa in defining and interpreting Sikhism.[^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p28-269][^cite_note-Mandair_2013_85–86-270][^cite_note-fenech273-271]

Sanatan Sikhs led by [Khem Singh Bedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khem_Singh_Bedi) – who claimed to be a direct descendant of Guru Nanak, Avtar Singh Vahiria and others supported a more inclusive approach which considered Sikhism as a reformed tradition of Hinduism, while Tat Khalsa campaigned for an exclusive approach to the Sikh identity, disagreeing with Sanatan Sikhs and seeking to modernise Sikhism.[^cite_note-fenech273-271][^cite_note-Oberoi1994-268][^cite_note-Mandair2013p82-272] The Sikh Sabha movement expanded in north and northwest Indian subcontinent, leading to more than 100 Singh Sabhas.[^cite_note-fenech273-271][^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p28-269] By the early decades of the 20th century, the influence of Tat Khalsa increased in interpreting the nature of Sikhism and their control over the Sikh [gurdwaras](482849.md).[^cite_note-fenech273-271][^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p28-269][^cite_note-Oberoi1994-268] The Tat Khalsa banished Brahmanical practices including the use of the *[yagna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yajna)* fire,[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] replaced by the *[Anand Karaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anand_Karaj)* marriage ceremony in accordance with Sikh scripture, and the idols and the images of Sikh gurus from the Golden Temple in 1905, traditions which had taken root during the administration of the *[mahants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singh_Sabha_Movement#Background)* during the 1800s.[^cite_note-275] They undertook a sustained campaign to standardise how Sikh gurdwaras looked and ran, while looking to Sikh scriptures and the early Sikh tradition[^cite_note-perplexed85-276] to purify the Sikh identity.[^cite_note-277]

The spiritual successors of the Singh Sabha include the [Akali movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akali_movement) of the 1920s, as well as the modern-day [Shiromani Gurdwara Parbandhak Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiromani_Gurdwara_Parbandhak_Committee) (SGPC), a gurdwara administration body, and the [Akali Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akali_Dal) political party.[^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p30-278]

#### Partition of India



Demolished Gurudwara with partially burnt skeletal remains of victims lying on the floor, in the aftermath of the [Rawalpindi Massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rawalpindi_Massacres) of March 1947, which preceded the partition and were the first partition-related violence in Punjab to show clear signs of [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing)

Sikhs participated and contributed to the decades-long Indian independence movement in the first half of the 20th century. Ultimately when the British Empire recognised independent India, the [land was partitioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) into Hindu-majority India and Muslim-majority Pakistan (East and West) in 1947. According to Banga, the partition was a watershed event in Sikh history.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–100-279][^cite_note-Shani2007p86-280] The Sikhs had historically lived in northwestern region of Indian subcontinent on both sides of the partition line ("[Radcliffe Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radcliffe_Line)"). According to Banga and other scholars, the Sikhs had strongly opposed the [Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-India_Muslim_League) demands and saw it as "perpetuation of Muslim domination" and anti-Sikh policies in what just a hundred years before was a part of the Sikh Empire. As such, Sikh organisations, including the [Chief Khalsa Dewan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Khalsa_Diwan) and [Shiromani Akali Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiromani_Akali_Dal) led by [Master Tara Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_Tara_Singh), condemned the [Lahore Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Resolution) and the movement to create Pakistan, viewing it as inviting possible persecution; the Sikhs largely thus [strongly opposed the partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_the_partition_of_India).[^cite_note-KudaisyaYong2004-281] During the discussions with the colonial authorities, Tara Singh emerged as an important leader who campaigned to prevent the partition of colonial India and for the recognition of Sikhs as a third community.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–100-279]

When partition was announced, the newly created line divided the Sikh population. Along with Hindus, Sikhs suffered organised violence and riots against them in West Pakistan. As a result, Sikhs moved en masse to the Indian side, leaving behind their property and holy sites.[^cite_note-Abid2014-282] However, the anti-Sikh violence was not one-sided. As Sikhs moved to the eastern side of the partition line, they engaged in reprisals against Muslims there, forcing them into Pakistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–100-279][^cite_note-Mann2014p81-283] Before the partition, Sikhs constituted about 15% of the population in West Punjab, the majority being Muslims (55%). The Sikhs were the economic elite in West Punjab, however. They had the largest representation in West Punjab's aristocracy, and there were nearly 700 Gurdwaras and 400 educational institutions that served the interests of the Sikhs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–103-284] Prior to the partition, there were a series of disputes between the majority Muslims and minority Sikhs, such as on the matters of [jhatka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jhatka) versus [halal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halal) meat, the disputed ownership of [Gurdwara Sahidganj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaheed_Ganj_Mosque) in Lahore which Muslims sought as a mosque and Sikhs as a Gurdwara, and the insistence of the provincial Muslim government on switching from Indian [Gurmukhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmukhi) script to Arabic-Persian [Nastaliq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nastaliq) script in schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–100-279] During and after the [Simla Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Conference) in June 1945, headed by Lord Wavell, the Sikh leaders initially expressed their desire to be recognised as a third community, but ultimately relegated these demands and sought a United India where Sikhs, Hindus and Muslims would live together, under a Swiss-style constitution. The Muslim League rejected this approach, demanding that the entire Punjab should be granted to Pakistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–104-285] The Sikh leaders then sought the original partition instead, and the Congress Working Committee passed a resolution in support of partitioning Punjab and Bengal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–104-285][^cite_note-Mann2014p81-283]



[Sikh Light Infantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Light_Infantry) personnel march past during the [Republic day parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Republic_Day_parade) in New Delhi, India.

Between March and August 1947, a series of riots, arson, plunder of Sikh and property, assassination of Sikh leaders, and killings in Jhelum districts, Rawalpindi, Attock and other places led to Tara Singh calling the situation in Punjab a "civil war", while [Lord Mountbatten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Mountbatten) stated "civil war preparations were going on."[^cite_note-Abid2014-282] The riots had triggered the early waves of migration in April, with some 20,000 people leaving northwest Punjab and moving to Patiala.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga2017104–105-286][^cite_note-Shani2007p86-280] In Rawalpindi, 40,000 people became homeless. The Sikh leaders made desperate petitions, but all religious communities were suffering in the political turmoil. Sikhs constituted only 4 million out of a total of 28 million in Punjab, and 6 million out of nearly 400 million in India; they did not constitute the majority, not even in a single district.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga2017104–105-286][^cite_note-Wolpert2010p9-287]

When the partition line was formally announced in August 1947, the violence was unprecedented, with Sikhs being one of the most affected religious community both in terms of deaths, as well as property loss, injury, trauma and disruption.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga2017108–111-288][^cite_note-Mann2014p81-283] Sikhs and Muslims were both victims and perpetrators of retaliatory violence against each other. Estimates range between 200,000 and 2 million deaths of Sikhs, Hindus and Muslims.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga2017108–111-288][^cite_note-Mann2014p81-283] There were numerous rapes of and mass suicides by Sikh women, they being taken captives, their rescues and above all a mass exodus of Sikhs from newly created Pakistan into newly independent India. The partition created the "largest foot convoy of refugees recorded in \[human] history, stretching over 100 kilometer long", states Banga, with nearly 300,000 people consisting of mostly "distraught, suffering, injured and angry Sikhs". Sikh and Hindu refugees from Pakistan flooded into India, Muslim refugees from India flooded into Pakistan, each into their new homeland.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga2017108–111-288][^cite_note-Wolpert2010p9-287]

#### Khalistan



Sikhs in [London](17867.md) protesting against the Indian government

In 1940, a few Sikhs such as the victims of [Komagata Maru in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komagata_Maru_incident) proposed the idea of Khalistan as a buffer state between an independent India and what would become Pakistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–103-284] These leaders, however, were largely ignored.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBanga201799–100-279][^cite_note-Shani2007p86-280] The early 1980s witnessed some Sikh groups seeking an independent state named [Khalistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalistan) carved out from India and Pakistan. The [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple) and Akal Takht were occupied by various militant groups in 1982. These included the [Dharam Yudh Morcha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharam_Yudh_Morcha) led by [Jarnail Singh Bhindranwale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarnail_Singh_Bhindranwale), the Babbar Khalsa, the AISSF and the National Council of Khalistan.[^cite_note-Chima2008p85-289] Between 1982 and 1983, there were [Anandpur Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anandpur_Resolution) demand-related terrorist attacks against civilians in parts of India.[^cite_note-horowitz482-290] By late 1983, the Bhindranwale led group had begun to build bunkers and observations posts in and around the [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple), with militants involved in weapons training.[^cite_note-Chima2008p85-289] In June 1984, the then [Prime Minister of India](24452.md) [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) ordered Indian Army to begin [Operation Blue Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Blue_Star) against the militants.[^cite_note-Chima2008p85-289] The fierce engagement took place in the precincts of Darbar Sahib and resulted in many deaths, including Bhindranwale. It also resulted in the destruction of the Sikh Reference Library, which was considered a national treasure that contained over a thousand rare manuscripts[^cite_note-mann_114-291] and the Akal Takht. Numerous soldiers, civilians and militants died in the cross fire. Within days of the Operation Bluestar, some 2,000 Sikh soldiers in India mutinied and attempted to reach Amritsar to liberate the [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple).[^cite_note-Chima2008p85-289] Within six months, on 31 October 1984, Indira Gandhi's Sikh bodyguards [Satwant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satwant_Singh) and [Beant Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beant_Singh_\(assassin\)) [assassinated her](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Indira_Gandhi). The assassination triggered the [1984 anti-Sikh riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_anti-Sikh_riots).[^cite_note-horowitz482-290] According to Donald Horowitz, while anti-Sikh riots led to much damage and deaths, many serious provocations by militants also failed to trigger ethnic violence in many cases throughout the 1980s. The Sikhs and their neighbours, for most part, ignored attempts to provoke riots and communal strife.[^cite_note-horowitz482-290]

Estimates as of 2019 state that Sikhism has some 25–30 million followers worldwide.[^cite_note-297] A 2020 estimate by Charles Preston gives a figure of 29,254,000 of Sikhs worldwide.[^cite_note-298] According to [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), a think tank and research group based in Washington, DC, over 9-in-10 Sikhs are in [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_India), but there are also sizeable Sikh communities in the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_the_United_States), the [United Kingdom](7940789.md), and [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Canada).[^cite_note-299] Within India, the Sikh population is found in every [state and union territory](375986.md), but it is predominantly found in the northwestern and northern states. Only in the state of [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_India) do Sikhs constitute a majority (58% of the total, per [2011 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Census_of_India)).[^cite_note-census2011sikhs-292] In addition to Punjab, the states and union territories of India where Sikhs constitute more than 1.5% of its population are [Chandigarh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandigarh), [Haryana](14189.md), [Delhi](37756.md), [Uttarakhand](1429154.md), and [Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md), all of which are in the northern half of India.[^cite_note-census2011sikhs-292]

Canada is home to the largest proportion of Sikhs, as a ratio of the country's total population, in the world, at 2.1%.[^cite_note-canadareligion2021-293] Within Canada, Sikhs form 5.9% of the total population in the western province of [British Columbia](3392.md), representing the third-largest Sikh proportion amongst all global [administrative divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions), behind only Punjab and Chandigarh in India. British Columbia, [Manitoba](18926.md), and [Yukon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yukon) hold the distinction of being three of the only four [administrative divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions) in the world with Sikhism as the second-most followed religion among the population.[^cite_note-ProvincePopulation-302]

Prior to the [1947 partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) of [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj), millions of Sikhs lived in what later became [Pakistan](24751.md). Likewise, Sikhism was founded in what is now Pakistan, and some of the gurus were born near [Lahore](125315.md) and in other parts of Pakistan. During the partition, Sikhs and Hindus left the newly created Muslim-majority Pakistan and mostly moved to Hindu-majority India—with some moving to Muslim-majority [Afghanistan](737.md)[^cite_note-303]—while numerous Muslims in India moved to Pakistan.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] According to 2017 news reports, only about 20,000 [Sikhs remain in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Pakistan), and their population is dwindling (0.01% of the country's estimated 200 million population).[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307]

### Sikh sects

Main article: [Sects of Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sects_of_Sikhism)



[Ravidassia Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravidassia) Gurdwara, Birmingham

Sikh sects are sub-traditions within Sikhism that believe in an alternate lineage of gurus, or have a different interpretation of the Sikh scriptures, or believe in following a living guru, or hold other concepts that differ from the orthodox Khalsa Sikhs.[^cite_note-Syan2014p170-308][^cite_note-Takhar2014p350-309] The major historic sects of Sikhism have included Udasi, Nirmala, Nanakpanthi, Khalsa, Sahajdhari, Namdhari Kuka, Nirankari, and Sarvaria.[^cite_note-Oberoi1994p24-310]

Sikhs originally had only five orders, or [sampradas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampradaya) (not to be confused as deviant sects). These include:

[Nihangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihang) – the Sikh [Panth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panth)'s warriors or armed troops.[^cite_note-\:5-311] There are two main groups within this order: Buddha Dal, or the army of veterans, and Tarna Dal, or the army of youth. There are other smaller sub-orders connected to these two.[^cite_note-\:5-311] The president of Buddha Dal, previously always served as the president of the [Akaal Takht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akal_Takht), which has jurisdiction over all things pertaining to the [Akaali Nihang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihang) order.[^cite_note-\:2a-10]



Sant Singh Khalsa, a white convert to Sikhism, authored the most widely used translation of the Guru Granth Sahib.

[Nirmalas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirmala_\(sect\)) – scholars. Composed texts as well as traditionally studying a wide range of [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_religions) and some non-Indian literature. They converse with other [Dharmik pathways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma) as well.[^cite_note-312] The tenth guru also institutionalised them. Bhai Daya Singh Ji Samparda and Bhai Dharam Singh Ji Samparda, two of the [Panj Pyare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panj_Pyare) or cherished ones of the tenth guru, founded two Nirmala orders.[^cite_note-313]

[Udasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Udasi) – an ascetic group that historically looked after [Gurdwaras](482849.md) and carried out [missionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary) activity. Although not promoting it to others, certain of their practices depart from the majority of Sikh beliefs.[^cite_note-\:3-314] [Baba Sri Chand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Chand), the eldest Sahibzada (son) of the first guru, [Guru Nanak Dev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak), founded the order.[^cite_note-\:3-314] Their Gurdev is Baba Sri Chand.[^cite_note-\:2a-10]

[Sevapanthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevapanthi_\(Sikh_Order\)) – philanthropists who engage in charitable work/seva, or selfless service, without expecting payment.[^cite_note-\:4-315] They also work on academic projects. Bhai Kahnaiya, a Sikh of the ninth and tenth guru, served as the first head of the order[^cite_note-316] and is renowned for his wartime medical assistance to wounded enemy soldiers.[^cite_note-\:4-315] Very few of them exist today. The environment in which they lived and with which they interacted was a predominantly Muslim one.[^cite_note-\:4-315]

[Gyaaniyan Samparda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damdami_Taksal) – the university of Sikhi, whilst technically not an order, it essentially serves as one.[^cite_note-Nirbhai_Singh_1990_1–3-317] Made up from individuals belonging to all of the above sects. Many branches within this order.



[Namdhari Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namdhari), also called the *Kuka* Sikhs are a sect of Sikhism known for their crisp white dress and horizontal *pagari* (turban).

[^cite_note-britsikhssects-318]

[^cite_note-HaarKalsi2009p10-177]

&#x20;Above: Namdhari singer and musicians.

The early Sikh sects were [Udasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Udasi) and [Minas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mina_\(Sikhism\)) founded by Baba [Sri Chand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Chand) – the elder son of [Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak), and [Prithi Chand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prithi_Chand) – the elder son of [Guru Ram Das](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Ram_Das) respectively, in parallel to the official succession of the Sikh gurus. Later on [Ramraiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramraiya) sect, founded by [Ram Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_Ram_Rai), grew in [Dehradun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dehradun) with the patronage of Aurangzeb.[^cite_note-FenechMcLeod2014p260-319] Many splintered Sikh communities formed during the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) era. Some of these sects were financially and administratively supported by the Mughal rulers in the hopes of gaining a more favourable and compliant citizenry.[^cite_note-Takhar2014p350-309][^cite_note-FenechMcLeod2014p260-319]

After the collapse of Mughal Empire, and particularly during the rule of Ranjit Singh, Udasi Sikhs protected Sikh shrines, preserved the Sikh scripture and rebuilt those that were desecrated or destroyed during the Muslim–Sikh wars. However, Udasi Sikhs kept idols and images inside these Sikh temples.[^cite_note-Oberoi1994p78-219][^cite_note-Fenech2014p375-320] In the 19th century, [Namdharis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namdhari) and [Nirankaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirankari) sects were formed in Sikhism, seeking to reform and return to what each believed was the pure form of Sikhism.[^cite_note-fenech273-271][^cite_note-SinghFenech2014p28-269][^cite_note-Mandair_2013_85–86-270]

All these sects differ from Khalsa orthodox Sikhs in their beliefs and practices, such as continuing to solemnise their weddings around fire and being strictly vegetarian.[^cite_note-britsikhssects-318][^cite_note-HaarKalsi2009p10-177] Many accept the concept of living gurus such as Guru [Baba Dyal Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_Dyal_Singh). The Nirankari sect, though unorthodox, was influential in shaping the views of Tat Khalsa and the contemporary-era Sikh beliefs and practices.[^cite_note-britsikhsects-321][^cite_note-322] Another significant Sikh sect of the 19th century was the [Radhasoami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radhasoami) movement in Punjab led by Baba Shiv Dyal.[^cite_note-HaarKalsi2009p9-323] Other contemporary era Sikhs sects include the [3HO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3HO), formed in 1971, which exists outside India, particularly in North America and Europe.[^cite_note-HaarKalsi2009p9-323][^cite_note-verne560-324][^cite_note-325]

### Sikh castes

Further information: [Sikhism and caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_and_caste)



[Ravidassia Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravidassia) Gurdwara, Nasinu, Fiji Established in 1939



[Nagar Kirtan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagar_Kirtan) in [Bangalore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangalore)

According to Surinder Jodhka, the state of Punjab with a Sikh majority has the "largest proportion of [scheduled caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalit) population in India". Although decried by Sikhism, Sikhs have practiced a caste system. The system, along with [untouchability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untouchability), has been more common in rural parts of Punjab. The landowning dominant Sikh castes, states Jodhka, "have not shed all their prejudices against the lower castes or [dalits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalits); while dalits would be allowed entry into the village gurdwaras they would not be permitted to cook or serve langar." The Sikh dalits of Punjab have tried to build their own gurdwara, other local level institutions and sought better material circumstances and dignity. According to Jodhka, due to economic mobility in contemporary Punjab, castes no longer mean an inherited occupation, nor are work relations tied to a single location.[^cite_note-326] In 1953, the government of India acceded to the demands of the Sikh leader, [Master Tara Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tara_Singh_\(activist\)), to include Sikh Dalit castes in the list of scheduled castes.[^cite_note-Harish_K._Puri_2004-327] In the [Shiromani Gurdwara Prabandhak Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiromani_Gurdwara_Prabandhak_Committee), 20 of the 140 seats are reserved for low-caste Sikhs.[^cite_note-Harish_K._Puri_2004-327]

The 2011 Indian Census recorded 5,390,484 Scheduled Caste individuals identifying as Sikhs in Punjab, out of the state's total Scheduled Caste population of 8,860,179, representing 33.68% of Punjab's total Sikh population (16,004,754).[^cite_note-328] Over 60% of Sikhs belong to the [Jat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jat_Sikh) caste,[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330] which is an agrarian caste. Despite being very small in numbers, the mercantile [Khatri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khatri) and [Arora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arora) castes wield considerable influence within the Sikh community. Other common Sikh castes include [Sainis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saini), [Ramgarhias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramgarhia) (artisans), [Brahmins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmins), [Ahluwalias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahluwalia_\(caste\)) (formerly brewers), [Rajputs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajput),[^cite_note-331] [Rai Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rai_Sikh) (Rai), [Kambojs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamboj) (rural caste), [Labanas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labana), [Kumhars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumhar) and the two Dalit castes, known in Sikh terminology as the [Mazhabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazhabi) (the Chuhras) and the [Ravidasias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravidasia) (the Chamars).[^cite_note-332]

### Sikh diaspora

Further information: [Sikhism in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_India), [Sikh diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_diaspora), and [Sikhism by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_by_country)



Sikhs celebrating [Vaisakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi) in [Toronto, Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toronto,_Canada)

Worldwide, there are 30 million Sikhs,[^cite_note-Britannica-333][^cite_note-334] which makes up 0.4% of the world's population. Approximately 75% of Sikhs live in [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab,_India), where they constitute 57.7% of the state's population. Large communities of Sikhs migrate to the neighbouring states such as Indian State of Haryana which is home to the second largest Sikh population in India with 1.1 million Sikhs as per 2001 census, and large immigrant communities of Sikhs can be found across India. However, Sikhs only comprise about 1.7% of the Indian population.[^cite_note-Singh_Kalsi_2007_12-335]

Most Sikhs outside India live in the [core Anglosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Core_Anglosphere), with 771,790 in [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Canada) (2.1% Sikh),[^cite_note-canadareligion2021-293][^cite_note-336] 524,140 in the [United Kingdom](7940789.md) (0.9% Sikh),[^cite_note-337] 280,000 in the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_the_United_States) (0.1% Sikh),[^cite_note-357] 210,400 in [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Australia) (0.8% Sikh),[^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359] and 40,908 in [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_New_Zealand) (0.9% Sikh).[^cite_note-New_Zealand_Sikh_population-360] While these communities are over 125 years old, most Sikhs in the West are first, second, or third-generation immigrants. As of the [2021 Canadian Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Canadian_Census), more than half of Canada's Sikhs can be found in one of four cities: [Brampton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brampton,_Ontario) (163,260),[^cite_note-bramptoncity2021-361] [Surrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrey,_BC) (154,415),[^cite_note-surreycity2021-362] [Calgary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calgary) (49,465),[^cite_note-calgarycity2021-363] and [Abbotsford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbotsford,_BC) (38,395).[^cite_note-abbotsfordcity2021-364]

Sikhs also migrated to East Africa, the Middle East, and Southeast Asia. These communities developed as Sikhs migrated out of Punjab to fill in gaps in imperial labour markets.[^cite_note-365]

## Prohibitions in Sikhism

Further information: [Prohibitions in Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prohibitions_in_Sikhism) and [Diet in Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diet_in_Sikhism)

Four major transgressions:[^cite_note-366]

- [Hair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hair) removal – Hair [cutting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cutting), trimming, removing, [shaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaving), [plucking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plucking_\(hair_removal\)), [threading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threading_\(epilation\)), [dyeing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyeing_hair), or any other alteration from any body part is strictly forbidden.

  [^cite_note-367]

- Eating [Kutha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutha_meat) meat.

  [^cite_note-368]

  &#x20;This is the absolute minimum required by all [initiated Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa). Many Sikhs refrain from eating [non-vegetarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-vegetarian) food, and believe all should follow this diet. This is due to various social, cultural, political, and familial aspects. As such, there has always been major disagreement among Sikhs over the issue of eating [non-vegetarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-vegetarian) food. Sikhs following the rahit (code of conduct) of the [Damdami Taksal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damdami_Taksal) and [AKJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhand_Kirtani_Jatha) also subscribe to this view. The [Akali Nihangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihang) have traditionally eaten meat and are famous for performing [Jhatka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jhatka).

  [^cite_note-369]

  [^cite_note-Mahaprashad-370]

  [^cite_note-Sikh_Goat_Sacrifice-371]

  [^cite_note-Sikh_Goat_Sacrifice2-372]

  &#x20;Thus, there is a wide range of views that exist on the issue of a proper "Sikh diet" in the [Panth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panth). It has been stipulated that meat slaughtered via the Muslim ([Halal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halal)) or Jewish ([Shechita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shechita)) methods is against Sikh dogma and principles.

  [^cite_note-373]

  [^cite_note-374]

  &#x20;The [Akal Takht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akal_Takht) represents the final authority on controversial issues concerning the Sikh [Panth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panth) (community or collective). The [Hukamnama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hukamnama) (edict or clarification), issued by [Akal Takht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akal_Takht) [Jathedar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jathedar) [Sadhu Singh Bhaura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jathedar_Sadhu_Singh_Bhaura) dated 15 February 1980, states that eating meat does not go against the code of conduct of the Sikhs. [Amritdhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalsa) Sikhs can eat meat as long as it is [Jhatka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jhatka) meat.

  [^cite_note-Singh_2001_89-375]

- [Adultery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adultery): Cohabiting with a person other than one's spouse (sexual relations with anyone who you are not married to).

  [^cite_note-376]

  [^cite_note-377]

  [^cite_note-378]

  [^cite_note-379]

  [^cite_note-380]

- [Intoxication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intoxications) – Consumption of [tobacco](30942.md) and [intoxicants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intoxicants) ([hemp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemp), [opium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opium), [liquor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquor), [narcotics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcotic), [cocaine](7701.md), etc.) is not allowed.

  [^cite_note-381]

  [^cite_note-382]

  [^cite_note-383]

  &#x20;[Cannabis is generally prohibited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_and_Sikhism), but ritually consumed in edible form by some Sikhs.

  [^cite_note-SinghFenech2014-384]

  [^cite_note-SinghHawley2012-385]

  &#x20;Some Sikh groups, like the [Damdami Taksal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damdami_Taksal), are even opposed to drinking [caffeine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caffeine) in [Indian tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea). Indian tea is almost always served in Sikh [Gurudwaras](482849.md) around the world. Some [Akali Nihang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihang) groups consume [cannabis-containing](1481886.md) shaheedi degh (ਭੰਗ), purportedly to help in [meditation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meditation).

  [^cite_note-386]

  [^cite_note-387]

  [^cite_note-388]

  &#x20;*Sūkha parshaad* (ਸੁੱਖਾ ਪ੍ਰਰਸਾਦ), "Dry-sweet", is the term Akali Nihangs use to refer to it. It was traditionally crushed and consumed as a liquid, especially during festivals like [Hola Mohalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hola_Mohalla). It is never smoked, as this practice is forbidden in Sikhism.

  [^cite_note-389]

  &#x20;In 2001, [Jathedar Santa Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jathedar_Santa_Singh), the leader of [Budha Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budha_Dal), along with 20 chiefs of [Nihang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihang) sects, refused to accept the ban on consumption o&#x66;*&#x20;shaheedi degh* by the apex Sikh clergy of [Akal Takht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akal_Takht) – in order to preserve their traditional practices.

  [^cite_note-390]

  &#x20;According to a 2011 [BBC](19344654.md) article, "Traditionally they also drank shaheedi degh, an infusion of cannabis, to become closer with God".

  [^cite_note-391]

  &#x20;Baba Santa Singh was excommunicated and replaced with Baba Balbir Singh, who agreed to shun the consumption of [bhang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhang).

  [^cite_note-392]

Other mentioned practices to be avoided, as per the Sikh Rehat Maryada:

- Piercing of the nose or ears for wearing ornaments is forbidden for Sikh men and women.

  [^cite_note-393]

  &#x20;However, this is a point of contention as it was common for Sikh men and women to wear during the [Sikh Misl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Misl) period.

  [^cite_note-394]

- [Female infanticide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Female_infanticide): A Sikh should not kill their daughter; nor should they maintain any relationship with a killer of a daughter.

  [^cite_note-395]

- A Sikh shall not [steal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theft), form dubious associations or engage in [gambling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gambling).

  [^cite_note-396]

- It is not proper for a Sikh woman to wear a [veil](68897.md), or keep her face hidden.

  [^cite_note-397]

- Sikhs cannot wear any token of any other faith, nor participate in idol worship ([idolatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry)) according to the Guru Granth Sahib. Sikhs must not have their head bare or wear [caps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap). They also cannot wear any [ornaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornament_\(art\)) piercing through any part of the body.

  [^cite_note-398]

- Hereditary priest – Sikhism does not have priests, as they were abolished by Guru Gobind Singh.

  [^cite_note-autogenerated2-399]

  &#x20;The only position he left was a [Granthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granthi) to look after the Guru Granth Sahib; any Sikh is free to become Granthi or read from the Guru Granth Sahib.

  [^cite_note-autogenerated2-399]

[ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9781351900102](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9781351900102). Since the Sikh concept of the divine is panentheistic, the divine is always greater than the created universe, its systems such as karma and samsara, and all phenomena within it. In Sikhism, due to the sovereignty of God, the doctrines of Nadar and Hukam override all systems, both concepts reinforcing panentheism. Hence one becomes a jivanmukt only in accordance with the Hukam.

[^cite_note-2]: &#x20;Singh, Pashaura; Mandair, Arvind-Pal Singh (2023). *The Sikh world*. London New York: Routledge. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9780429848384](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9780429848384). In looking at the teachings of the Gurus as a whole, it seems that Lourdunathan overstates the degree to which Sikh scripture is anti-monistic. Guru Nanak famously referred to the world as a "palace of smoke" (GGS: 138) and made countless references to the idea of maya (Illusion). While the Gurus did not teach a radical nondualism, it is perhaps more accurate to suggest that some aspects of Sikh thought constitute a qualified nondualism (in which Creator and Creation are part of the same whole) (GGS: 125) or panentheism (in which the Creator pervades the natural world) (GGS: 24), while many others are monotheistic, including passages in Japji Sahib, where God is described as the King of Kings (GGS: 6). These different interpretations lend themselves to varying understandings of the relationship between the natural world and divinity.

[ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9780191062773](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9780191062773). From the rest of this introduction to the Guru Granth Sahib, and from Guru Nank's compositions, it is a monotheistic, rather than a monist, view of God which emerges.

The main thrust of Kabir's mission was to unite Hindus and Muslims in a common quest for god realisation. 'Hindus and Muslims have the same god, he held. 'God is the breath of all breath ... Look within your heart, for there you will find \[God] ... All men and women in the world are his living forms.' Although many of his sayings had a strong Hindu flavour in them—presumably because of Ramananda's influence—he made no distinction between Hinduism and Islam Similarly, though he usually referred to god as Hari or Rama, he used those words as synonyms of god, and not as the names of particular deities. 'I am not Hindu nor Muslim; Allah-Ram is the breath of my body,' he stated, and went on to declare that All that lives and dies, they are all one. The this and that haggling, is done.

[^cite_note-123]: &#x20;Susanne, Scholz (2013). *God Loves Diversity and Justice*. Lexington Books. p. 16. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-7391-7318-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-7391-7318-3). The Sikh Divine comprises every imaginable theological ideal! Hindu, Buddhist, Tantric, and Islamic views that were current in medieval India come together in the wide-ranging literary spectrum of the GGS. The stereotypical oppositions between the Indic and Abrahamic worldviews of the day are transcended: "Some call it Rama, some call it Khuda; some worship it as Vishnu, some as Allah" (GGS: 885). Interestingly, even the atheistic Buddhist Nirvana is not omitted: "Itself Nirvana, It itself relishes pleasures" (GGS: 97). "God" or "gods" or "no god" alike are recognized as part of the infinite One! "Always, always you alone are the One Reality – *sada sada tun eku hai*" (GGS: 139). Persian terminology is used to emphasize the unity of being: "*asti ek digari kui ek tui ek tui* – Only the One is, there is none other; Only you, you only" (GGS: 144). Again, "*hindu turk ka sahib ek*  – Hindus and Muslims share the One sovereign" (GGS: 1158). (The term "Turk" referred to all Muslims in this period.) Since everything is a manifestation of That One being, all the manifestations would be a part of it. No god, no body, and no thing is excluded from this all pervasive being

University of Tennessee Press. pp. 27–28. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-57233-214-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-57233-214-0). To the extent that Sikhism has received attention in general treatments on world religions, it has typically received brief coverage (Juergensmeyer 1979, 1993). For some time it was treated as a descendant of both Hinduism and Islam, but today the preferred approach among Western academics is to place Sikhism "within the context of fifteenth and sixteenth-century India, especially the milieu of the medieval sants" and to view it as arising primarily within a Hindu milieu while recognizing some Islamic influences (Juergensmeyer 1993, 14; McLeod 1976). This scholarship places Sikhism in the general context of Bhakti religion, which, though it originated earlier, had become popular throughout India by the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries. Bhakti stresses devotion as the route to God (as opposed, for example, to the paths of knowledge or good works).

[^cite_note-201]: &#x20;McLeod, William Hewat (1968). *Guru Nanak and the Sikh Religion*. p. 161. It is accordingly incorrect to interpret the religion of Guru Nanak as a synthesis of Hindu belief and Islam. It is indeed a synthesis, but one in which Islamic elements are relatively unimportant. The pattern evolved by Guru Nanak is a reworking of the Sant synthesis, one which does not depart far from Sant sources as far as its fundamental components are concerned.

Abingdon-on-Thames, England: [Taylor & Francis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_&_Francis). pp. 111–. [^cite_note-210]: &#x20;Lewis, James R. ["SOME UNEXAMINED ASSUMPTIONS IN WESTERN STUDIES OF SIKHISM"](https://web.archive.org/web/20220127153440/https://sikhinstitute.org/recent_res/ch18.html). *Institute of Sikh Studies*. Archived from [the original](https://sikhinstitute.org/recent_res/ch18.html) on 27 January 2022. Retrieved 19 August 2026. If one examines survey books (especially textbooks) on world religions and/or general works on Asian religion, one almost invariably finds that Sikhism, if mentioned at all, is usually presented as "a hybrid of two old religions, Islam and Hinduism." There are, however, some curious variations on the syncretism theme. One finds, for example, that some authors assert that Nanak "stands in a closer relation to Hinduism" . while other authors assert that Nanak "leaned rather more to Islam than to Hinduism." Similarly, in opposition to writers who explain Sikhism as being "an offshoot of Hinduism", one can discover writers who assert that in Sikhism "there is little doubt that the Muslim source predominates". Still other authors emphasize that Sikhism, because of its syncretic character, "is not in any absolute sense new", an opinion not shared by scholars who stress the originality of Guru Nanak" over Sikhism's supposed syncretism. And it would probably be possible to find many other such examples of disagreement. Thus Hindu/Muslim syncretism, which many writers on religion appear to regard as an axiomatic and "obvious" category for beginning any analysis of the Sikh religion, turns out to be extremely ambiguous, This peculiar state of affairs leads us to ask deeper kinds of questions about Sikh syncretism, such as, why has this question attracted so much attention? and, what ultimately, is at stake in this issue? .

[^cite_note-KudaisyaYong2004-281]: &#x20;Kudaisya, Gyanesh; Yong, Tan Tai (2004). *The Aftermath of Partition in South Asia*. Abingdon-on-Thames, England: [Routledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Routledge). p. 100. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-134-44048-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-134-44048-1). No sooner was it made public than the Sikhs launched a virulent campaign against the Lahore Resolution. Pakistan was portrayed as a possible return to an unhappy past when Sikhs were persecuted and Muslims the persecutor. Public speeches by various Sikh political leaders on the subject of Pakistan invariably invoked images of atrocities committed by Muslims against Sikhs and of the martyrdom of their *gurus* and heroes. Reactions to the Lahore Resolution were uniformly negative and Sikh leaders of all political persuasions made it clear that Pakistan would be 'wholeheartedly resisted'. The Shiromani Akali Dal, which had a substantial following among rural Sikhs, organized several well-attended conferences in Lahore to condemn the Muslim League. Master Tara Singh, leader of the Akali Dal, declared that his party would fight Pakistan 'tooth and nail'. Not be outdone, other Sikh political organizations, rival to the Akali Dal, namely the Central Khalsa Young Men Union and the moderate and loyalist Chief Khalsa Dewan, declared in equally strong language their unequivocal opposition to the Pakistan scheme.

[^cite_note-Abid2014-282]: &#x20;Abid, Abdul Majeed (29 December 2014). ["The forgotten massacre"](https://nation.com.pk/29-Dec-2014/the-forgotten-massacre). *The Nation*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201202231234/https://nation.com.pk/29-Dec-2014/the-forgotten-massacre) from the original on 2 December 2020. Retrieved 7 December 2020. On the same dates, Muslim League-led mobs fell with determination and full preparations on the helpless Hindus and Sikhs scattered in the villages of Multan, Rawalpindi, Campbellpur, Jhelum and Sargodha. The murderous mobs were well supplied with arms, such as daggers, swords, spears and fire-arms. (A former civil servant mentioned in his autobiography that weapon supplies had been sent from NWFP and money was supplied by Delhi-based politicians.) They had bands of stabbers and their auxiliaries, who covered the assailant, ambushed the victim and if necessary disposed of his body. These bands were subsidized monetarily by the Muslim League, and cash payments were made to individual assassins based on the numbers of Hindus and Sikhs killed. There were also regular patrolling parties in jeeps which went about sniping and picking off any stray Hindu or Sikh. ... Thousands of non-combatants including women and children were killed or injured by mobs, supported by the All India Muslim League.

They feared that the formation of a Sikh-majority province would further consolidate the power of the already dominant landowning Jat Sikhs, which would make their position in the rural society of Punjab even more vulnerable. After the 1966 reorganization of Punjab, the Jat Sikhs constituted nearly 60 percent of the population and the Hindus around 38 percent.

Tradiyionally the majority of their followers have come from the Jat-Sikhs. According to an 1881 census, 66% of Sikhs were Jats, followed by Ramgarhia (carpenter) Sikhs (6.5%), and Chamar (cobbler and weaver) Sikhs (5.6%). The Khatris' share was only 2.2%.' Although Sikhs are prominently identified by a set of diacritical features, which they are supposed to follow according to the Rahatnama (the Sikh code of conduct), the Jat-Sikhs do not always observe them strictly.

[^cite_note-357]: &#x20;While the [U.S. Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Census) does not ask about religion,<sup>[\[327\]](#cite_note-338)</sup> 70,697 Americans (or 0.02% of the total population) declared Sikh as their ethnicity in the [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_U.S._census).<sup>[\[328\]](#cite_note-339)</sup> In the [2021 Canadian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Canadian_census), 194,640 Canadians declared Sikh as their ethnicity while 771,790 Canadians declared Sikh as their religion, indicating that the Sikh American population may be around 280,329, or 0.08% of the total population.<sup>[\[329\]](#cite_note-340)</sup> The [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Census_Bureau) estimated the adult Sikh American population at 78,000 in 2008.<sup>[\[330\]](#cite_note-341)</sup> The [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) estimated the Sikh American adult population to be 140,000 and the total population at 200,000 in 2012 while the World Religion Database at Boston University estimated the American Sikh population to be at 280,000 in 2012.<sup>[\[331\]](#cite_note-342)</sup><sup>[\[332\]](#cite_note-How_Many_U.S._Sikhs-343)</sup> Sikh organisations like the [Sikh Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Coalition) and [American Sikh Congressional Caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Sikh_Congressional_Caucus) estimate the Sikh American population to be as high as 1,000,000, but do not provide any sources for these figures;<sup>[\[333\]](#cite_note-344)</sup><sup>[\[334\]](#cite_note-345)</sup><sup>[\[332\]](#cite_note-How_Many_U.S._Sikhs-343)</sup> 500,000 nevertheless remains the most cited Sikh American population size in news media.<sup>[\[335\]](#cite_note-346)</sup><sup>[\[342\]](#cite_note-353)</sup> With 1% of [Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans#Religion) being Sikh, and 90.7% of Sikh Americans being Asian American, the American Sikh population can be estimated around 280,000 in 2021.<sup>[\[343\]](#cite_note-354)</sup><sup>[\[344\]](#cite_note-355)</sup><sup>[\[345\]](#cite_note-356)</sup>
